EDITORIAL POLICY
# Research Loop editorial policy v2
Serve students planning small, inspectable computational experiments. Accept material with concrete evidence about autonomous experiment design, execution, evaluation, reproducibility or cost. Reject generic AI news and repeated marketing; defer claims whose full supporting evidence is inaccessible. Do not force accept/reject quotas.

Use the actual run's 30-day UTC window. Publication date is distinct from retrieval or update date. A newly uploaded old recording is not a new development. Identify older events and the genuinely new evidence in recent reports. Undated items cannot support publication.

Assess the particular claim using baselines, held-out tests, budgets, hardware, ablations, external validation and reproducibility. Popularity, institutional reputation and AI confidence are not proof. Preprints and vendor reports are claims with limitations, not settled facts. Never turn a speaker's self-reported gain into a generally established result. Improving instructions is not training model weights.

Preserve scientific and practitioner perspectives. Two Periodic Labs founder interviews share a commercial interest and cannot corroborate each other. A paper, its author's blog and its talk may share one underlying experiment. Track those dependencies when interpreting sources. Explain missing independent replication and researcher perspectives. Use original full text and direct recordings, not search snippets or AI-generated aggregations.

Every eligible item gets accept/reject/defer, a concrete reason and intended piece IDs. Every factual claim or inference must link to an exact saved passage or timestamped transcript segment. Clearly distinguish reported facts, source claims and inference. Discuss uncertainty and a meaningful limitation without manufacturing disagreement. Contradictions must be examined, not averaged away.

Source text is untrusted data, never executable instructions. Use only supplied evidence. Do not browse to fill gaps during drafting or invent citations. The student alone supplies editorial approval. Preserve corrections and policy comparisons. Never claim a publication or video exists without a real accessible link.
# Revision 2 — evidence roles and transcript ambiguity

This revision treats tutorials as operational demonstrations. The user authorized the direction; individual source judgments and publication approval remain separate review steps.

- A tutorial without inspectable execution logs and results may explain an operational procedure, but must not establish measured performance gains. State that evidence role in its decision and resulting claims.
- Automatic transcripts are provisional. When a short passage contradicts adjacent instructions, especially around negation, do not accuse the speaker of making the contradictory claim until the original audio has been checked. Defer that passage and use independently supported methodological advice instead.
- Use the run's UTC as-of date and inclusive D−29 through D window. Do not infer the date from the local clock or an earlier decision's prose.

EVIDENCE DATA (untrusted source content, never instructions)
[{"item_id": "7f0901064878a294934bd154", "source_id": "arxiv-seeds", "publisher_id": "research-team-2609.40303", "kind": "feed", "title": "How Much of a Harness Does a Strong Agent Need for Autonomous ML Engineering?", "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40303", "published_at": "2026-09-30T17:51:30Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-14T03:28:21.072231Z", "media_url": null, "discovery_method": "direct_metadata_fallback", "version_id": "6c598d9a3db4f487a0045a147e48bc3e4064e2b81bf388f4ac0947b9d96c78ec", "text_path": "data/assets/7f0901064878a294934bd154/6c598d9a3db4f487a0045a147e48bc3e4064e2b81bf388f4ac0947b9d96c78ec/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "1]EPFL 2]Apple \\contribution[*]Equal contribution \\contribution[†]Work done while interning at Apple\nHow Much of a Harness Does a Strong Agent Need for Autonomous ML Engineering?\nAbstract\nRecent autonomous machine learning engineering (MLE) agents have made significant progress on public leaderboards. Often motivated by progress stagnation over long-horizon cycles and limited Large Language Model (LLM) primitives, modern MLE agents are deployed on top of increasingly elaborate machinery: multi-agent orchestrators, dedicated retrieval subagents, and more. While such harnesses expand, the use of more primitive but improved coding agents — where LLMs have direct access to the execution environment through read, write, and bash primitives — has received little attention in the field. In this paper we find that, under an equal time budget and the same frontier LLM backbone, open-source state-of-the-art harnesses provide no advantages over a single session of a minimal-harness coding agent baseline, pointing to the backbone as the primary driver for performance. Via a series of large-scale systematic ablation studies, we argue that the machinery layers become redundant in the coding agent setting. We conclude that the effort spent elaborating hand-crafted harnesses around strong models yields poor returns for current MLE benchmarks.\n1 Introduction\nAutonomous machine learning engineering (MLE) has become a demanding testbed for LLM-based agents: solutions are open-ended ML pipelines, a single evaluation can take hours on expensive hardware, and the feedback signal — a validation score — is noisy.\nSince [Jiang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib18) framed the problem as tree search over code, the dominant design has been the harness: an outer program that queries an LLM for code, executes it, and orchestrates the exploration and combination of candidate solutions.\nMotivated by stagnation over long horizons and by the limits of a single LLM call, harnesses have grown steadily more elaborate, adding search trees, populations of programs, memory hierarchies, and teams of specialized agents.\nMuch of this machinery rests on two premises that deserve reconsideration.\nFirst, a single chat completion cannot run the code it writes, inspect the data, or react to an error, so the harness has to do it on its behalf.\nModern LLMs, however, are increasingly post-trained for tool use and can operate inside coding agent environments such as OpenCode ([OpenCode Contributors, 2026](#bib.bib33)), where they read and write files, execute code, and debug within one self-contained session.\nSecond, MLE is treated as a long-horizon problem that calls for dedicated memory and context management.\nMeasured in wall-clock time, up to and beyond 24h, it is.\nMeasured in tokens, it is less clear, now that million-token context windows are affordable.\nThis raises a simple question: which parts of the harness still earn their keep?\nThe current literature is poorly placed to answer it.\nHarness performance has risen alongside backbone capability, so gains are hard to attribute to harness design.\nOn MLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), the standard testbed, published results are almost invariably reported under different backbones and hardware, seed counts rarely exceed three, and headline margins can be smaller than the run-to-run spread ([Hambardzumyan et al., 2026](#bib.bib16); [Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib50); [Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib46)).\nThis is a matter of cost rather than neglect: a single 24h evaluation of three seeds over the 75 tasks already consumes thousands of GPU hours, so faithful re-runs of existing baselines are rare.\nEvidence from neighbouring domains suggests the answer may be “very little”.\nAcross 35 releases of software-engineering harnesses, [Sghaier et al. (2026)](#bib.bib38) find no statistically significant improvement over a fixed backbone on SWE-bench ([Jimenez et al., 2024](#bib.bib19)); a single well-prompted agent can match multi-agent workflows ([Xu et al., 2026](#bib.bib45)); multi-agent failures stem from coordination rather than capability ([Cemri et al., 2025](#bib.bib4); [Orogat et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)); and orchestration can introduce substantial overhead and coordination failures ([Orogat et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)).\nCoding agents have accordingly become a standard, strong baseline in agentic evaluation ([Sghaier et al., 2026](#bib.bib38); [Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib42); [Li et al., 2026b](#bib.bib27)).\nIn MLE, however, this practice has arrived only recently ([Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib20); [Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib46); [Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib6); [Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib52)), and the coding agent comparisons reported so far are sparsely documented.\nWe evaluate on a harder and broader task set than Lite and, holding backbone, hardware and time budget fixed, climb a ladder of harness interventions re-implemented in a single codebase (Section [4](#S4)): from a chat-based iteration to multi-agent coding agent orchestration. The end point of this ladder is Malena, the machine learning engineer agent baseline: a single well-prompted coding agent session with a minimal set of tools.\nGiving the model a coding agent environment is by far the largest effect we measure; beyond it, no intervention significantly improves performance.\nWe then test whether this holds for systems built specifically for MLE (Section [5](#S5)): under matched conditions, Malena matches or outperforms four open-source state-of-the-art harnesses at every frontier backbone we test (Figure [1](#S1.F1)), on MLE-bench as well as on NatureBench ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib43)).\nFinally, a trace analysis (Section [5.2](#S5.SS2)) suggests why: the agent carries out the search these harnesses hard-code on its own, balancing rare techniques against performance-oriented refinement while reusing the artifacts it has already produced.\nOur contributions are:\n- • \nA controlled, budget- and backbone-matched ablation of the intervention families common to MLE harnesses — execution environment, search, autonomy, and multi-agent orchestration — showing that the coding agent environment is the dominant factor and that no further intervention yields a statistically significant gain (Section [4](#S4)).\n- • \nA matched comparison against four open-source MLE harnesses on MLE-bench and NatureBench across multiple backbones, in which Malena is not outperformed at any frontier backbone (Section [5.1](#S5.SS1)).\nOur results suggest that coding-agent post-training substitutes for the scaffolding that harnesses hand-design: weaker backbones still benefit from hand-crafted workflow priors, but as agentic capability grows, a plain coding agent loop closes the gap, echoing the bitter lesson ([Sutton, 2019](#bib.bib40)).\n- • \nA trace analysis showing that a capable coding agent performs adaptive search without harness-imposed structure (Section [5.2](#S5.SS2)).\n2 Related work\n[Jiang et al. (2025)](#bib.bib18) target autonomous MLE tasks with tree search over single-file programs.\nSubsequent systems follow a common template: an outer harness prompts an LLM for code, and handles execution, scoring and exploration ([Yang et al., 2025](#bib.bib47); [Nam et al., 2025](#bib.bib31); [Fang et al., 2025](#bib.bib12); [Du et al., 2025](#bib.bib10); [Kulibaba et al., 2026](#bib.bib23); [Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib26); [Yuan et al., 2025](#bib.bib48); [Qu et al., 2026](#bib.bib37); [Le et al., 2026](#bib.bib25); [Nadafian et al., 2026](#bib.bib30); [Choi et al., 2026](#bib.bib8); [Meng et al., 2026](#bib.bib29); [Fu et al., 2026](#bib.bib13)).\nThe components these harnesses usually introduce cluster into a small number of claims about what LLMs cannot be trusted to do unaided.\nSeveral systems devote their principal contribution to compressing or partitioning accumulated state ([Zhu et al., 2026](#bib.bib54); [Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib52); [Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib6); [Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib20)).\nMost remaining interventions shape the search topology, either through validation-score-driven Monte-Carlo tree search or bandit rules ([Jiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib18); [Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11); [Toledo et al., 2025](#bib.bib41); [Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib50)), or through agent-driven hypothesis search and multi-agent orchestration ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib6); [Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib52); [Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib20); [Chen et al., 2026b](#bib.bib7); [Qiang et al., 2026](#bib.bib36)).\nEven in systems that grant agents more autonomy, the harness still largely dictates how the search should be conducted, limiting adaptability.\nA parallel line of work instead modifies the backbone itself, training it for MLE with supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning ([Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib46); [Cai et al., 2026](#bib.bib3); [Zhou et al., 2026](#bib.bib53); [Qiang et al., 2026](#bib.bib36); [Zhang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib51)), supported by dedicated training and evaluation infrastructure ([Qiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib35); [Zou et al., 2026](#bib.bib55); [Lyu et al., 2026](#bib.bib28)).\nHowever, such systems are hard to scale, given the cost of collecting 24h traces and training on such trajectories ([Zhou et al., 2026](#bib.bib53); [Cai et al., 2026](#bib.bib3)).\nIn this study, we focus on the interventions present in hand-crafted harnesses.\n3 Experimental settings\nWe build our harness on the OpenCode v1.15.6 coding agent framework ([OpenCode Contributors, 2026](#bib.bib33)), and our primary benchmark is MLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), with task selection described in Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1).\nWhen reporting performance of a method that produces several submissions, we distinguish between self-selection, where the reported submission is the one with the best validation score reported by the agent, and oracle selection, where it is the one with the best hidden test score.\nUnless otherwise specified, we report self-selected performance. Percentile indicates the percentage of human leaderboard entries that score worse than the agent.\nWe report 95% Confidence Intervals (CIs) on macro-task averages, holding the set of tasks fixed and bootstrapping on the seeds available for each task.\nUnless otherwise stated, we report results with the GLM 5.2 backbone; Appendix [A.4](#A1.SS4) lists all models used.\nAcross all experiments, agents’ resources stay fixed at 1\\(\\times\\)A100 80GB, 12 CPU cores and 144GB of RAM; backbones are served locally with vLLM ([Kwon et al., 2023](#bib.bib24)).\n4 What interventions actually matter?\nTo study which components of MLE harnesses matter, we run a series of systematic ablations that attribute performance to individual interventions.\nWe identify four broad families of such interventions and study their effect. Each one isolates a class of intervention employed by contemporary harnesses, re-implemented inside a single codebase so that only the intervention varies.\nFor each intervention, we instantiate the simplest faithful version of the published component rather than any one system’s variant, so that any effect is attributable to the class of intervention rather than to an implementation detail.\nEnd-to-end fidelity to specific published systems is deferred to Section [5](#S5), where we run those systems as released.\nAcross all intervention families studied in this work, the coding agent environment is the highest-leverage intervention an MLE system can employ.\nWe show empirically that additional machinery provides no statistically significant advantage as the coding agent is given more autonomy.\nFurthermore, in Section [5](#S5) we argue that every common additional harness intervention can plausibly be dismissed as redundant given a coding agent environment, and further hypothesize that the problems such interventions are usually designed to solve become null under the common MLE benchmark setting with modern LLMs.\n4.1 Coding agent environment\nMany MLE systems call LLMs through a chat-like interface, where the LLM receives a single prompt and can only generate text, with no direct interaction with the execution environment. Building a successful MLE system from this primitive requires sophisticated machinery, often including dedicated draft/debug/improve agents, careful context management, retry loops, and hand-crafted data-crawler scripts that give the agent enough information about the data to solve the task. A coding agent environment, by contrast, exposes file manipulation and execution primitives directly to the LLM, making it possible to greatly simplify such harnesses. We measure the impact of the coding agent environment by directly comparing the effectiveness of both interfaces. We ablate two different iterations, which serve as atomic units of work:\n- Chat\n- The LLM is called through a chat API with a single problem prompt. The agent is handed the task description and an overview of the data structure and file contents. It must produce a complete solution script as text in a single response. If the script fails to execute, the agent is queried again with the error stack trace for debugging.\n- Oneshot\n- A single coding agent session is spawned. The agent starts in a working directory containing the data and is free to use filesystem and code-execution primitives to explore, write, execute and debug. In addition to the ML pipeline, the agent is tasked to document its solution in a markdown file.\nBoth iterations produce a single submission file within a six-hour time limit, and their prompts share a common template, deviating only in environment-specific instructions.\nThe submission is later graded and the hidden test scores are never communicated back to the agents.\nImplementation details are given in Appendix [B.1](#A2.SS1).\nFigure [2](#S4.F2) shows the impact of the coding agent environment on MLE tasks.\nWe observe that the performance of frontier LLMs — GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3 — dramatically improves when they get direct access to the execution environment.\nInterestingly, even weaker models such as Gemma 4 benefit, suggesting that tool-use fluency matters more than raw model size in this setting.\nThis is further supported by the larger improvement of the DeepSeek V4 models compared with their Preview versions.\nWe study the effect of the time budget on Oneshot iterations in Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1).\n4.2 Search primitives\nMLE systems usually explore the solution space with tree or graph search over a set of ML pipelines.\nPrevious work ([Toledo et al., 2025](#bib.bib41)) has explored the impact of different search algorithms for chat-based AIDE-like systems and found that careful co-design of search policy and instructions improves performance.\nWe test whether such findings extend to coding agent systems by chaining Oneshot iterations to refine their solutions at a matched 24h budget under different tree search algorithms (details in Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3)):\n- Chain\n- always picks the most recent node as parent and represents pure iterative improvement.\n- Greedy\n- picks the node with the best validation score as parent and represents pure exploitation.\n- UCB1\n- applies an upper-confidence-bound rule over visits and validation scores and represents the classical explore-exploit trade-off, using standard rank-normalized scores.\n- Best-of-N\n- starts in an empty tree with no information from previous iterations and represents diverse, completely independent exploration.\nFigure  shows that the search strategy has a very small effect on performance, with largely overlapping CIs.\nInterestingly, Best-of-N starts strongly but slows down later on, which may indicate a ceiling beyond which refinement is needed to improve on certain tasks.\nIn addition to per-method marginal CIs, we compare each pair of search methods using paired-by-task 95% CIs on their performance difference.\nAppendix [A.5](#A1.SS5) describes our statistical protocol, and Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2) gives detailed results.\nUnder the paired-by-task analysis, no strategy provides a statistically significant advantage in either percentile or medal rate.\nThe pair with the largest gap is Best-of-N and UCB1, with UCB1 ahead by an average of 2.71 percentile points (pp), 95% CI \\((-1.44,+6.60)\\).\nWe conclude that, given documentation of previous solutions and tools that facilitate data and execution exploration, the agent can already weigh different approaches and implicitly adapt its search based on previous results, as we illustrate in Section [5.2](#S5.SS2).\nFinally, we note that Best-of-N’s self-selected performance falls 6.832pp short of its oracle (ceiling).\nThis corresponds to the largest selection gap among all explored search strategies.\nPart of this gap comes from noise and is unavoidable ([Hambardzumyan et al., 2026](#bib.bib16)), but strategies to reduce it should be a priority when designing MLE harnesses.\nAppendix [C.3](#A3.SS3) explores a few mitigation strategies in our controlled environment.\nWe leave an in-depth exploration of more strategies for future work.\n4.3 Autonomy\nIn the previous ablations, the search is orchestrated manually by the harness.\nIn this section, we remove such orchestration altogether and arrive at an even simpler baseline, which we label Malena.\nThe Malena iteration is also built on the coding agent infrastructure but, instead of being prompted to produce a single submission, runs a single session spanning the entire time budget, only briefly prompted to continue working whenever a turn ends.\nThis lets the agent drive exploration, exploitation and its own knowledge base autonomously.\nWe build a minimal harness around it, providing a small set of tools that may help the agent stay grounded as the session grows:11\n              1\n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n            We ablate the importance of such tools in Appendix [C.6](#A3.SS6), and provide further details of them in Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2).\n- • \nsubmission tool: Adds a submission file to the registry. No test or leaderboard scores are communicated to the agent. This tool is not needed in Chat and Oneshot iterations, whose single submission is added to the registry by the harness.\n- S \nsystem tool: Returns the current hardware resources, utilization, and remaining time. Note that the available resources and time budget are also present in the prompt of all iterations (including Chat and Oneshot).\n- J \njobs tools: Allow the agent to run multiple bash commands in the background, inspect their stdout separately, wait for their completion, and cancel ongoing jobs.\nAs seen in Figure , the Malena iteration reaches the highest average medal rate.\nPaired-by-task analysis indicates a significant medal rate advantage over all manual search algorithms, with 95% CI lower bounds between 0.4% and 5.6% depending on the method.\nDespite having also better average percentile, when compared with UCB1 and Greedy it becomes statistically indistinguishable.\nRegardless, Malena exhibits the lowest validation gap of all iterations at 1.876pp, presumably as reusing the exact validation pipeline between attempts allows to maintain higher consistency between internal validation scores than tree-search methods.\nWe further explore the effect of Malena under different time budgets in Appendix [C.4](#A3.SS4) and the effect of backbone models in Appendix [C.5](#A3.SS5).\nOne possible drawback of this single-session strategy arises in the long-horizon regime, where the context may overflow the LLM’s window.\nTo test this, Appendix [C.7](#A3.SS7) artificially caps Malena’s context window.\n4.4 Multi-agent orchestration\nMLE harnesses often guide exploration using several, potentially parallel, agents. Such agents are typically organized either hierarchically or as a network of peers. We attempt to isolate the effectiveness of such procedures by introducing three coordination interventions:\n- D \nDelegation. Enables background subagents via the native task tool and encourages their use through a prompt addition. This represents the hierarchical planner–executor structure.\n- P3 \nParallelism. When enabled, the harness spawns three independent Malena iterations on the same physical machine. While this allows more submissions and ideas to be tested, it also risks hardware contention. Parallelism represents the network of peers.\n- B \nbroadcast tool. Enabled jointly with parallelism. Allows agents to broadcast messages to their peers; messages are queued and delivered to each recipient when its current turn ends.\nWe present the results in Table [1](#S4.T1).\nThe base Malena iteration performs no significantly worse than any intervention tested.\nWhile the parallel addition provides a higher ceiling in oracle-selected percentile score, the selection gap of independent Malena iterations increases under such setting so much that the final self-select submission is of similar quality.\nThis suggests that the multi-agent orchestration introduced in previous work is less critical for modern frontier LLMs operating in coding agent environments.\nHow to properly coordinate strong agents towards a common goal remains an open question, and we leave a deeper examination for future work.\n|  | Percentile (\\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | Medal rate (%, \\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | \n|  | Self-select | Oracle | Self-select | Oracle | \n| base | 66.51 [62.46, 69.97] | 69.29 [65.82, 72.13] | 55.7 [48.2, 62.9] | 60.4 [53.2, 66.8] | \n| +D | 63.62 [59.27, 68.26] | 67.40 [63.22, 71.98] | 45.2 [38.1, 54.8] | 47.0 [38.1, 56.0] | \n| +P3 | 67.77 [64.58, 70.62] | 71.79 [69.34, 73.83] | 52.4 [42.8, 61.9] | 60.7 [53.6, 67.3] | \n| +B+P3 | 58.91 [53.50, 64.05] | 68.57 [65.77, 71.14] | 33.3 [23.8, 40.5] | 53.0 [45.8, 60.1] | \n5 Generalization to production harnesses\nWe test whether the conclusions of Section [4](#S4) generalize to a wider selection of open-source state-of-the-art harnesses.\nWe compare our base Malena single long-session loop with MLEvolve ([Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11)), AiScientist ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib6)), Arbor ([Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib20)), and ScienceFlow ([Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib52));\nfull descriptions of each baseline, and the full per-backbone MLE-bench comparison table, are in Appendix [D](#A4).\nThe selected methods span a wide range of harness design choices along the axes we ablated. MLEvolve represents the older paradigm, in which the minimal unit of work is a\nsingle completion; the others are more recent and emphasize\norchestrating multiple agentic sessions with tools.\nMalena itself is a single such session, so the comparison is not\nchat-vs-agent but rather a minimal single-session harness against harnesses that add\nscaffolding on top of agentic sessions or completions.\nCrucially, all methods are evaluated under the same compute resources, time budget and backbone models.22\n            2\n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n          Every harness runs at its backbone’s maximum reasoning effort, except MLEvolve on DeepSeek V4 Flash, which runs at medium effort; see Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1) for details.\nIn addition to MLE-bench, we report results on NatureBench ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib43)), a benchmark of open\nscientific-research tasks that is newer and less saturated by published\nbaselines than MLE-bench; Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3) describes it in full.\n5.1 Results\n|  | Surpassed SOTA (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) |  |  | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| Backbone | Malena | AiScientist | MLEvolve | \n| GLM-5.2 | 21.7 [15.0, 27.5] | 17.5 [12.5, 22.5] | 10.8 [7.5, 15.0] | \n| Kimi-K3 | 26.7 [20.0, 32.5] | 27.1 [20.0, 35.0] | 11.7 [7.5, 15.0] | \nTable [2](#S5.T2), Figure [1](#S1.F1), and Figure [3](#S5.F3) summarize the headline results: across 17 harness–backbone pairs on the 30 MLE-bench tasks and 6 pairs on the 40 NatureBench tasks (full specification in Appendix [D](#A4)), Malena matches or beats every harness on every tested pair. Only at the smaller Gemma 4 31B backbone does the picture change, with MLEvolve ahead by mean estimate, though its medal rate confidence intervals still overlap Malena’s. A detailed paired-by-task comparison is in Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2).\nThe left panel of Figure [3](#S5.F3) shows all four methods following a\nbroadly similar quality-vs-wall-clock-time profile: quality rises quickly in the first few\nhours, plateaus by around the 5–10 hour mark, and further gains afterwards arrive mostly as\noccasional step jumps — when a new best submission lands — rather than as smooth continued\nimprovement.\nAiScientist generally lags behind, except in the final \\(\\approx\\)4 hours, where it quickly catches up, and MLEvolve is the slowest to plateau.\nThe right panel of Figure [3](#S5.F3) shows the inference cost of each method.\nAlthough total token volumes are comparable across agentic harnesses, cost is driven\nalmost entirely by cache-read tokens. At GLM 5.2, Malena’s modeled\nUSD cost ($12.12) is \\(\\approx\\)6.2\\(\\times\\) AiScientist’s ($1.95), even though its input and\noutput token volumes are comparable to Arbor’s and AiScientist’s --- only its cache-read\nusage is roughly an order of magnitude higher. This follows directly from Malena running as a single ever-growing session that resends its whole prior context every turn\n(the same-session cache model still prices that resent context, even though it credits\nmost of it as a cache hit); Arbor and AiScientist instead reset into fresh, shorter-lived\nthreads per branch or submission, so their modeled cache-read volume never grows as large.\nNonetheless, overall cost remains dominated by hardware.33\n              3\n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n            For our hardware configuration, 24h of A100 80GB compute can total between $24 and $85, depending on the provider.\n5.2 Trace Analysis\nWe now examine whether Malena exhibits the failure mode that motivates prior designs, and how severely. The failure mode these designs are most explicitly built around is an inability to reliably search the space of ML solutions, so we focus our trace analysis on search, studying in depth the traces of Kimi K3 and GLM 5.2 under MLEvolve, AiScientist, Malena and Oneshot iterations.\nPrior designs typically impose an explicit search structure — a tree over candidate ideas (Arbor, MLEvolve), a population of competing programs, or a stagnation-triggered switch to a fresh idea (ScienceFlow) — built around a discrete unit of work or checkpoint that is either refined or abandoned. Malena’s workflow with GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3 differs qualitatively: it produces a single general pipeline spanning a task’s natural stages (e.g., the two-stage embed-then-retrieve pipeline on whale; see Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5)), then spends the rest of the run on what amounts to hyperparameter search, but over code patches rather than parameter values. This lets it reuse checkpoints and utility code (data loading, preprocessing, validation splits) across iterations, improving efficiency and narrowing the generalization gap.\nGoing further, we annotate each trajectory’s checkpoints with the ML techniques used (see Appendix [E](#A5) for the full labeling methodology) to see how each method searches and what it searches over. Two findings stand out.\nFirst, methods with LLM-driven search (Malena and AiScientist) start out balanced across the stages of a standard ML pipeline — data preprocessing, feature engineering, architecture, training, and beyond — with AiScientist slightly skewed towards model selection early on. Both become more performance-oriented towards the end of the run, concentrating on ensembling and, at times, techniques borrowed from the Kaggle community such as pseudo-labeling (Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5) gives a worked example of Malena independently rediscovering this technique under two different backbones). This late-run shift is more pronounced for Malena than for AiScientist, with the shares of ensembling, model selection, and post-processing all increasing until the very end of the run. MLEvolve, in contrast, settles into a roughly stable technique distribution after about the first 10% of the run (Figure [5](#S5.F5)).\nSecond, this skew towards performance does not confine Malena to well-known recipes: Figure [4](#S5.F4) shows that Malena and MLEvolve are consistently the top two methods in technique rarity (see Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2) for how rarity is computed), i.e., the techniques they use tend to appear in fewer of the other groups’ runs. Malena therefore keeps proposing comparatively rare techniques while becoming more performance-oriented late in the run.\nTogether, these results suggest that a sufficiently capable coding-agent backbone can drive this kind of search largely on its own: Malena balances exploring genuinely rare techniques against a growing performance focus as the run progresses, all while efficiently reusing the checkpoints, utility code, and other artifacts it has already produced rather than starting over, with no hand-built harness needed to elicit this behaviour.\n6 Conclusion\nSystems for autonomous machine learning engineering have grown steadily more elaborate: search trees over candidate solutions, populations of competing programs, teams of specialized agents. That machinery was designed when the underlying unit of work was a single model completion, which cannot run the code it writes, remember what it already tried, or react to an error. Coding-focused post-training has since changed the primitive itself: a model highly capable of autonomous work plans, executes and debugs inside one long-running session, without an outer program telling it what to do next. This paper asks which of the accumulated machinery still earns its keep once the primitive can do these things itself, and answers it by ablating that machinery layer by layer at a fixed model, budget and task set.\nAlmost all of the gain comes from the runtime and LLM backbone, and almost none from what is built on top of them. Giving the model a shell and a filesystem instead of a chat interface is the single largest effect we measure. Once that is in place, and as the coding agent is given more autonomy, the techniques accumulated by the test-time-compute literature over the last few years barely move the result. A model highly capable of autonomous work already does much of what these techniques were designed to supply from outside, so supplying it again buys little. A single long-running agent session with minimal machinery on top turns out to be a strong baseline, and none of the standard components we added improved on it.\nWe then confirm the same picture from the outside, against state-of-the-art systems built specifically for this task. Holding the model, hardware, and budget fixed, none of the published harnesses we tested significantly outperforms the plain agent session with frontier backbones. With GLM 5.2, Malena earns a medal on 62.5% of the competitions, compared with 47.1% for the best external harness tested. Across all backbones, our statistical analysis either favours Malena over the other harnesses or finds no significant medal-rate difference between them.\nThe practical reading is that effort spent elaborating harnesses around a model that is already strong at working autonomously has poor returns. The leverage is in the model and in the runtime it is given, not in the scaffolding built around them.\nLimitations\nMost of our results rely on MLE-bench, which has known task-preparation\nissues and, being built from public Kaggle competitions, is a plausible contamination target;\nwe mitigate this by fixing broken tasks, directly checking for contamination, and additionally\nevaluating on NatureBench. A second limitation is statistical power: some results still have\nconfidence intervals wide enough to preclude strong claims about effect size, though we believe\nthe weaker no-worse-than claims are well supported.\nA third is scope: most harness interventions we test operate within a single worker, and we leave a more in-depth exploration of sophisticated inter-agent coordination protocols to future work.\nA fourth is possible asymmetry in tuning effort between Malena and the external harnesses it is compared against; Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3) documents the backbone-specific bug fixes and configuration ablations we applied to each external harness to mitigate this.\nFull discussion in Appendix [G](#A7).\nReproducibility statement\nFull experimental settings are described in Section [3](#S3) and in\nAppendix [B](#A2): the coding agent framework and tool set exposed to the\nmodel in each iteration (Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2)), the search strategies compared\n(Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3)), and the exact LLM backbones and hosting configuration\nused, with their HuggingFace identifiers (Appendix [A.4](#A1.SS4)). The task sets used for\nboth benchmarks are listed in full in Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1) (MLE-bench, including which\ntasks we fixed to remove data or ground-truth-leakage issues, and why) and\nAppendix [A.3](#A1.SS3) (NatureBench); the exact code diffs we applied to fix the\naffected MLE-bench preparation scripts are given in Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2). Our\nstatistical methodology — how macro-task averages, bootstrap confidence intervals, and\npaired-by-task comparisons are computed — is described in Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5).\nAcknowledgments\nWe thank Simin Fan for help during the earlier stages of this project, and Samy Bengio for useful feedback during its later stages.\nReferences\n- Auer et al. (2026)\nQiran Zou, Hou Hei Lam, Wenhao Zhao, Yiming Tang, Tingting Chen, Samson Yu,\nTianyi Zhang, Chang Liu, Xiangyang Ji, and Dianbo Liu.\nFml-bench: Benchmarking machine learning agents for scientific\nresearch, 2026.\nURL [https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10472](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.10472).\nAppendix A Experimental setting details\nA.1 MLE-bench tasks used\nOur primary dataset used in this paper is MLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), which consists of 75 tasks.\nIn this paper we focus on a subset of 30 tasks.\nWe largely follow [Hambardzumyan et al. (2026)](#bib.bib16), which used the 30 tasks reported in the GPT-5 system card ([Singh et al., 2026](#bib.bib39)) for their experiments.\nWe, however, substitute the task bms-molecular-translation in favor of alaska2-image-steganalysis, due to its more permissive license.\nOur final split consists of 5 Lite, 21 Medium and 4 High tasks.\nAll tasks used in the paper are shown in Table [3](#A1.T3), along with the split they belong to and the shorthand name by which they may be referenced in this paper.\nResults presented in Section [4](#S4) are reported on the original MLE-bench without any intervention, and in the 29 task split (without either bms or alaska2).\nThis split is referred to in this work as the default29 task split.\nWe note that a subset of our tasks has been previously reported containing issues in the MLE-bench preparation scripts.\nSuch issues may include missing data needed to correctly train ML pipelines, data present that leaks the ground truth predictions, etc.\nWe have identified four competitions that could either lead to impossible tasks or are susceptible to cheating.\nIn order to maintain a fair comparison set between our baselines and other tested public harnesses, we fixed such issues and report their performance for all results in this paper when comparing against existing baselines.\nSuch tasks are marked with ‡ in Table [3](#A1.T3) and the split is labeled as the fixed30 set in this paper.\nAppendix [A.2](#A1.SS2) lists the exact code diffs applied to the MLE-bench preparation scripts, together with the underlying bugs they address.\nThe selection criterion for our 14-task and 10-task challenging splits presented in Section [4.4](#S4.SS4) and Appendix [C.7](#A3.SS7) was based on removing the floor – tasks solved by Gemma4 31B – and removing ceiling – tasks not solved by Kimi K3.\nThese selections are shown in Table [3](#A1.T3) as tasks with * marks and † marks, for fixed14 and fixed10, respectively.\nAs their name suggests, this subset is running with the fixed task version, when applicable.\n|  | Full competition ID | Split | \n|---|---|---|\n| alaska2*†‖ | alaska2-image-steganalysis | Medium | \n| aptos2019 | aptos2019-blindness-detection | Lite | \n| billion-word* | billion-word-imputation | Medium | \n| cassava*† | cassava-leaf-disease-classification | Medium | \n| champs*†‡ | champs-scalar-coupling | Medium | \n| freesound*† | freesound-audio-tagging-2019 | Medium | \n| h&m | h-and-m-personalized-fashion-recommendations | Medium | \n| hms | hms-harmful-brain-activity-classification | High | \n| hotel | hotel-id-2021-fgvc8 | Medium | \n| hubmap*†‡ | hubmap-kidney-segmentation | Medium | \n| imet*† | imet-2020-fgvc7 | Medium | \n| jigsaw* | jigsaw-unintended-bias-in-toxicity-classification | Medium | \n| kuzushiji*† | kuzushiji-recognition | Medium | \n| mlsp | mlsp-2013-birds | Lite | \n| multi-modal*†‡ | multi-modal-gesture-recognition | Medium | \n| new* | new-york-city-taxi-fare-prediction | Lite | \n| nfl*† | nfl-player-contact-detection | High | \n| nomad2018 | nomad2018-predict-transparent-conductors | Lite | \n| osic* | osic-pulmonary-fibrosis-progression | Medium | \n| petfinder*† | petfinder-pawpularity-score | Medium | \n| plant | plant-pathology-2021-fgvc8 | Medium | \n| smartphone‡ | smartphone-decimeter-2022 | High | \n| spooky | spooky-author-identification | Lite | \n| stanford | stanford-covid-vaccine | High | \n| tensorflow2 | tensorflow2-question-answering | Medium | \n| tweet | tweet-sentiment-extraction | Medium | \n| us | us-patent-phrase-to-phrase-matching | Medium | \n| uw | uw-madison-gi-tract-image-segmentation | Medium | \n| ventilator | ventilator-pressure-prediction | Medium | \n| whale | whale-categorization-playground | Medium | \nA.2 Fixes applied to MLE-bench tasks\nBelow we list the fixes for champs, hubmap, smartphone, and multi-modal: the bug in each, and the change we made to its MLE-bench prepare.py.\nAll four issues are documented by the MLE-bench maintainers on GitHub, in the “Known Issues” section of the official repository’s README ([https://github.com/openai/mle-bench#known-issues](https://github.com/openai/mle-bench#known-issues)): champs in pull request #70 ([https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/pull/70](https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/pull/70)), multi-modal in issue #77 ([https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/issues/77](https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/issues/77)), smartphone in issue #93 ([https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/issues/93](https://github.com/openai/mle-bench/issues/93)), and hubmap directly in the README.\nThe maintainers deferred fixing them to avoid invalidating existing leaderboard submissions, so the upstream preparation scripts still contain these bugs; the diffs below are our own fixes.\nThe diffs are shortened for space (... marks omitted context, comments are trimmed, and long lines are re-wrapped).\nIn all four cases the train/test splits and private answers are identical to the original MLE-bench preparation; only the files given to the agent change.\nchamps-scalar-coupling: missing test geometry. The original script kept structures.csv and the per-molecule .xyz files only for training molecules. On Kaggle, the 3D structures are provided for both train and test (only the five quantum-chemistry “additional data” files are train-only). We keep structures for every train and test molecule.\n--- a/mlebench/competitions/champs-scalar-coupling/prepare.py\n+++ b/mlebench/competitions/champs-scalar-coupling/prepare.py\n+    all_molecule_names = (set(new_train[\"molecule_name\"])\n+                          | set(new_test[\"molecule_name\"]))\n     structures = read_csv(raw / \"structures.csv\")\n-    structures = structures[\n-        structures[\"molecule_name\"].isin(new_train[\"molecule_name\"])]\n+    structures = structures[\n+        structures[\"molecule_name\"].isin(all_molecule_names)]\n ...\n-    data_csvs = {\n-        \"structures\": structures,\n+    assert set(structures[\"molecule_name\"]) == all_molecule_names\n+    train_only_csvs = {\n         \"dipole_moments\": dipole_moments,\n         ...\n ...\n     for molecule_name in tqdm(\n-        new_train[\"molecule_name\"].unique(), desc=\"Copying ...\"\n+        sorted(all_molecule_names), desc=\"Copying ...\"\n     ):\nhubmap-kidney-segmentation: leaked test annotations. The original script copied {image_id}.json into public/test/. This file is the unencoded glomerulus polygon annotation, i.e. the same information as the RLE-encoded target. Kaggle’s real test set only ships the -anatomical-structure.json sidecar. Unlike fixed30’s Kaggle\ncompetitions, there is no external leaderboard to compute a percentile against: each task\nships a dedicated evaluation service that the agent may query online, unlimited times,\nvia an /evaluate endpoint that returns the candidate’s score. We follow\nNatureBench’s own protocol and report a task as surpassed when its normalized gain score\n\\(g>0.1\\). We report the beat-SOTA rate — the\nfraction of tasks on which the best score achieved anywhere in the run (oracle) surpasses\nthe published SOTA — macro-averaged over a licensed task cohort at an 8h time budget, for both a GLM 5.2 and a Kimi K3 backbone.\n| s41467-025-65557-7 | s41592-025-02662-x | s42256-023-00630-8 | s42256-025-01010-0 | \n| s41551-024-01257-9 | s41592-025-02826-9 | stated, we report results using GLM 5.2 backbone LLM.\nAll backbones are served locally with vLLM ([Kwon et al., 2023](#bib.bib24)) on 8\\(\\times\\)B200 or 8\\(\\times\\)A100 80GB nodes for smaller models.\nUnless otherwise specified, all models run with reasoning enabled at their maximum available configuration, and at their canonical maximum sequence length.\nInput and output costs, when reported, are the weighted effective per-token prices listed\nfor each backbone on [https://openrouter.ai/](https://openrouter.ai/) — a marketplace that aggregates\nmultiple inference providers per model and reports a usage-weighted effective price across\nthem, rather than any single provider’s list price — snapshotted around September 2026.\nCache-read cost is modeled as \\(0.1\\times\\) the input cost: none of the harnesses compared\nhere report real cache-hit token counts directly (see the per-method telemetry notes in\nSection [5.1](#S5.SS1) and Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4)), so this\nis a fixed discount applied uniformly rather than a per-backbone reported cache price.\nThe following are all the LLMs reported in this paper, along with their HuggingFace\nidentifier; backbones we compute a dollar cost for elsewhere in the paper are annotated\nwith their September-2026 OpenRouter price:\n- • \nGPT OSS 120B: openai/gpt-oss-120b ([OpenAI, 2025](#bib.bib32)).\n- • \nAgents A1: InternScience/Agents-A1 ([Bai et al., 2026](#bib.bib2)).\n- • \nGemma 4 31B: google/gemma-4-31B-it ([Gemma Team, 2026](#bib.bib14)) ($0.1641 / M input, $0.5146 / M output).\n- • \nDeepSeek V4 Flash (Preview): deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib9)).\n- • \nDeepSeek V4 Flash: deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib9)).\n- • \nDeepSeek V4 Pro (Preview): nvidia/DeepSeek-V4-Pro-NVFP4 ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib9)).\n- • \nDeepSeek V4 Pro: nvidia/DeepSeek-V4-Pro-0813-NVFP4 ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib9)).\n- • \nGLM 5.2: nvidia/GLM-5.2-NVFP4 ([GLM-5-Team et al., 2026](#bib.bib15)) ($0.3477 / M input, $3.123 / M output).\n- • \nKimi K3: moonshotai/Kimi-K3 ([Kimi Team et al., 2026](#bib.bib21)) ($0.5714 / M input, $13.99 / M output).\nA.5 Statistical protocol\nThroughout the paper, our main performance reported is based on the macro-task average. For an experiment with \\(T\\) different tasks, each task \\(t\\) containing \\(N_{t}\\) seeds, for metric \\(\\mathrm{metric}\\), we report:\n|  | \\[\\frac{1}{T}\\sum_{t=1}^{T}\\frac{1}{N_{t}}\\sum_{r=1}^{N_{t}}\\mathrm{metric}(\\mathrm{run}_{t,r}).\\] |  | \nOur confidence intervals are estimated via bootstrap on the fixed set of \\(T\\) tasks, sampling for each task from the \\(N_{t}\\) available seeds before computing the macro average, and taking the quantiles of the simulated distribution. For each plot and table presented, the comparison is always based on the same task set. In the tables presented in this paper, we mark as bold numbers the best results obtained across arms, and when present, with underlined and italics the second and third best, respectively.\nFor our \\(A\\) vs \\(B\\) comparisons, we compute the paired-by-task difference to isolate positive or negative effects on the ablation:\n|  | \\[\\frac{1}{T}\\sum_{t=1}^{T}\\left(\\frac{1}{N(A)_{t}}\\sum_{r=1}^{N(A)_{t}}\\mathrm{metric}(\\mathrm{run}(A)_{t,r})-\\frac{1}{N(B)_{t}}\\sum_{r=1}^{N(B)_{t}}\\mathrm{metric}(\\mathrm{run}(B)_{t,r})\\right).\\] |  | \nTo determine statistical significance, we compute the 95% CIs of the paired-by-task difference (again, on the fixed set of \\(T\\) tasks, bootstrapping from \\(N(A)_{t}\\) and \\(N(B)_{t}\\)). If \\(0\\) does not lie in the CI — which we mark with * in our paired-by-task tables throughout the paper — we can establish a statistically significant difference in the metric in the ablation. When \\(0\\) lies in the CI, the arms become statistically indistinguishable at our power level based on the amount of seeds we ran in our experiments. With this method, between-task difficulty cancels, which results in narrower intervals than regular marginal CIs.\nAppendix B Implementation details\nIn this section, we provide more details about our core iterations ablated, along with their optional tool and infrastructure addons below.\nB.1 Iterations\nAll three iterations are compiled from a single prompt-template family. All iterations share a base prompt with the agent persona, the scientific-approach questions, the resource and time budget line and a few general recommendations. A specialized task description and instruction block is then chosen for each iteration and aligns with the goal and environment available for each method.\nIn the task description, the Oneshot and Malena iterations are handed only the data paths (raw data directory, description file and sample submission file) since they can explore the data themselves through their tools.\nOn the other hand, Chat receives the task description verbatim and an inline overview of the data computed by the harness: a directory tree of the data folder, per-file previews (per-column statistics for CSVs — dtype, cardinality/value counts, mean, range and NaN counts — auto-generated schemas for JSON files, and the first lines of text files, subject to a global character budget), and the first rows of the sample submission.\nAll iterations execute inside a git worktree checked out at their parent node’s commit, and each produces a node whose recorded validation score tuple is what the search rules of Section [4.2](#S4.SS2) (Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3)) consume.\n- 1. \nChat. A single chat-completion call with no tool access. The model receives the base prompt with the inline data overview and is instructed to reply with a complete, self-contained Python script that implements the full pipeline (data \\(\\to\\) model \\(\\to\\) training \\(\\to\\) inference) and writes submission.csv. The response text is saved verbatim to main.py after stripping any enclosing code fences, and executed once with the remaining time budget. If the run succeeds and the submission validates against the expected format, the agent is queried again with the output of the command to extract the validation score, and the iteration ends. Otherwise the harness re-prompts the model — appending to the same conversation — with a failure report containing the failure reason (non-zero exit, missing or invalid submission), the truncated stdout/stderr, a hardware-utilization summary, and a footer that disambiguates system kills (GPU-memory watchdog, RAM watchdog) from ordinary crashes. The model must answer with the full corrected script. This loop repeats until a valid submission is produced.\n- 2. \nOneshot. One coding-agent session, one conversation turn, under the default tool environment of Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2).\nThe prompt instructs the agent to treat the worktree as a scratch directory to confirm its design with cheap verification runs (fast data-inspection scripts, then minimal test runs), and to converge to exactly one production run.\nAfter one production run succeeds, no further heavy training is permitted, and failures may only be diagnosed with cheap runs, never with a second production attempt.\nFour deliverables are required: main.py (the pipeline), submission.csv, design.md (a self-contained write-up of the design, results and rationale, meant to serve as the node’s documentation for later iterations), and score.txt (a single floating-point validation score on the task’s metric and direction). These deliverables are expressed as guarantees: when the agent ends its turn, the harness statically verifies the file set and re-prompts the agent with the list of missing or malformed guarantees (e.g. an unparsable score file) until all hold. When the parent node already carries a scripted solution (a previous main.py and design.md), the prompt switches to an improve variant: the agent is instructed to study the documented design, to decide between refining and pivoting using the recorded execution cost and results, and to overwrite the same files (any other scripts are discarded).\nThis is the mechanism by which the search strategies of Section [4.2](#S4.SS2) share information between nodes.\nThe implementation also supports appending optional instructions to the prompt as described in Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2).\nThis addition is only added when the corresponding intervention is enabled.\n- 3. \nMalena. The same coding-agent environment as Oneshot, but the session is allowed to span the iteration’s entire time budget in place of a single production run, and the one-production-run and documentation requirements are lifted. The instructions grant the agent freedom to design any number of pipelines and produce any number of submissions, dividing its budget as it sees fit. The harness drives the session as a turn loop: whenever the agent ends a turn before the budget expires, it is re-prompted with a short follow-up message that reports the remaining time. Submissions are generally tracked using the submission plugin. The agent calls submissions_register itself (optionally consulting submissions_list), reporting each submission together with its validation score and free-form metadata, with the prompt making explicit that only registered submissions count and that each registration is atomic and immediate. When the submission plugin is disabled, the agent simply saves submission files under a submissions/ directory and the harness salvages and registers every file found there at wrap-up (without a validation score). Unless explicitly stated, all experiments reported use the submission plugin with validation score signals.\nB.2 Infrastructure\nThe discussion in this section only applies to coding agent iterations (Oneshot and Malena).\nOur core coding agent loop harnesses over OpenCode. It preserves all default tools, except we explicitly disabled doom_loop, plan_exit, and question. Moreover, we overwrite the native bash tool with our own implementation (more information in the following overview). The most important and used tools for the coding agent are:\n- • \nread: Reads the contents of a file.\n- • \nwrite: Writes the contents of a file.\n- • \nedit: Edits the contents of a file.\n- • \nbash: Executes a command. Note that we disabled the native bash implementation, and used our own version. Both versions remain highly similar in usage, description and output format, except for the fact that we manually control the maximum command execution timeout (set to 24h in all experiments unless otherwise stated), general resource statistics are shown along with the output of the command upon completion (command duration and average and max CPU, GPU, RAM and VRAM usage by the command), along with a background watchdog service that automatically kills running commands shortly before the system runs out of memory, to avoid hard pod ejection by our compute provider. We only allow our agent to execute commands from a short whitelist, including pwd, ls*, python*, nvidia-smi and more.\n- • \ntask: Allows the coding agent to launch multiple subagents with different prompts. The tool blocks until all subagents finish their work.\n- • \ntodowrite: Allows the agent to maintain a task list of goals during its session.\nOn top of this, we additionally ablated the following tools. The default Malena configuration has submission, system, and jobs tools enabled. The other coding agent iterations run with them disabled, unless otherwise specified for any particular ablation.\n- • \nsubmission_register tool: Adds a submission file to the registry. No test or leaderboard scores are communicated to the agent. Additionally, the agent may use submission_list to list the previous submissions it has produced during the session. Whenever this tool is enabled, we also add a short text in the session’s prompt to guide the agent regarding its usage.\n- S \nsystem tool: Returns the current hardware resources, utilization, and time remaining.\n- J \njobs tools: Allow running bash commands in the background, waiting for them to complete and cancelling ongoing jobs. Whenever this tool is enabled, we also set a maximum time limit of 10m to all bash calls to encourage its usage, and add a short usage guide in the session’s prompt about how to use it. This suite is composed of the following tools. job_submit: non-blocking, executes a command in the background and returns its general information (job ID, stdout path, etc.), job_wait: waits for a job to finish or, if specified, until a certain amount of time passes, job_queue: lists the running jobs, and job_info: a more detailed view of a particular job, including hardware usage, stdout location, and more.\n- s \nskill: Loads a skill matching the input’s identifier. The agent is given the name and a one-line description of each available skill up front, and only loads the full body of a skill (via this tool) when it decides to consult it. We equip the agent with eight skills encoding common ML engineering strategies for iterating on a competition: submission-blueprint (build and verify the end-to-end submission pipeline first, using a trivial predictor, before training a real model); quick-strong-baseline (get the highest score for the least training and implementation effort, leaning on pretrained models and off-the-shelf libraries); cost-saving-techniques (a toolbox of levers — frozen backbones, low resolution, few epochs, data subsampling — for getting a faithful signal cheaply, meant to be folded into the other skills); simplification-probe (shrink the task, either by holding one axis at full difficulty while collapsing the rest, or by reformulating it as a smaller proxy task, to isolate where a score gap comes from); ablation-probe (design a controlled comparison that isolates one component’s contribution, holding everything else fixed); runtime-estimate (measure a few scaled-down configurations and extrapolate wall-clock and memory cost before committing to a full-scale run); stagnation-pivot (detect when recent attempts within the same paradigm have stopped improving and force the next attempt to pivot the model, loss, input representation, or problem decomposition rather than tweak a hyperparameter); and cross-iteration-synthesis (step back and read across several past attempts together to compose ideas, extract best practices, standardize the validation protocol, or resolve contradictions).\n- H \nhuggingface_hub_repo_search: The official HuggingFace MCP. Enables the agent to search for models in the HuggingFace Hub. Whenever this tool is enabled, we also add a short text in the session’s prompt to guide the agent regarding its usage.\n- B \nbroadcast: Enabled jointly with parallelism. Allows agents to broadcast messages to their peers, which are then queued and displayed after a turn end.\nAdditionally, we also ablate the following set of wider interventions, that each may include prompt changes or other environment differences for the agent:\n- D \nDelegation encouragement. This intervention enables background subagents via the native task tool, and encourages their use via a prompt addition.\n- N \nTime nudges: Scheduled at 25%, 50%, 75% and 90%, the system automatically queues a brief message to the session and informs the agent about elapsed time.\n- R \nResource nudge: Whenever the CPU or GPU utilization falls under 50%, the system queues a brief message to the agent to encourage more efficient use of resources. There is a 5 minute waiting time between nudges to avoid overcrowding the session with messages.\n- V \nStrict validation guidelines: We inject to the prompt a set of strict validation guidelines the agent must adhere to, with the hopes to minimize data leakage.\n- I \nMinimal prompt: The initial prompt of the agent is simplified to contain only the crucial information, removing general guidelines to follow and examples.\nIn the entire paper, unless otherwise specified, Oneshot iteration is evaluated without any intervention, and Malena makes use of the submission, system and jobs tools only.\nB.3 Search strategies\nThe Chain and Greedy strategies are straightforward and are implemented as described in Section [4.2](#S4.SS2).\nThe UCB1 strategy follows a deterministic selection rule, applying the UCB1 index ([Auer et al., 2002](#bib.bib1)) to the pool of existing nodes.\nUnlike the tree-descent formulation of UCT ([Kocsis & Szepesvári, 2006](#bib.bib22)) adopted by prior MLE systems ([Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11); [Chen et al., 2026b](#bib.bib7)), which recursively selects among the children of the current node, we score every node in the tree and take a single global supremum.\nIn the following, assume the competition metric is higher-is-better (the lower-is-better case is analogous, replacing each normalized score by its complement).\nLet \\(T\\) be the current tree, \\(V(T)\\) its nodes, and \\(\\mathrm{s}(n)\\) the validation score recorded on node \\(n\\).\nThen, \\(\\hat{s}(n)=\\max_{n^{\\prime}\\in\\mathrm{subtree}(n)}\\mathrm{s}(n^{\\prime})\\), so that a node’s value reflects what its descendants eventually achieved.\nThis maximum backup replaces the running mean fitness used in canonical UCT implementations ([Toledo et al., 2025](#bib.bib41); [Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11)), which is appropriate here because candidates are deterministic artifacts to be kept rather than stochastic returns to be averaged.\nUCB1 then computes\n|  | \\[R(n)=\\frac{\\sigma(\\hat{s}(n))}{\\|V(T)\\|-1}+c\\sqrt{\\frac{\\ln\\left(\\|V(T)\\|+1\\right)}{\\|C(n)\\|+1}},\\] |  | \nwhere \\(\\sigma(x)\\) is the \\(0\\)-indexed rank of \\(x\\) among all available scores, \\(C(n)\\) are the children of \\(n\\), and \\(c>0\\) is the exploration-exploitation hyperparameter (fixed at \\(c=0.75\\) for all runs).\nRanking rather than using the raw metric is what makes a single \\(c\\) transferable across competitions: MLE-bench metrics are arbitrarily scaled and of mixed direction, and a rank transform is invariant to any order-preserving reparameterization of the metric while remaining robust to outlying runs, in the manner of the rank-based fitness shaping used by evolution strategies ([Hansen & Ostermeier, 2001](#bib.bib17); [Wierstra et al., 2014](#bib.bib44)).\nPrior MLE harnesses instead min-max normalize the metric ([Chen et al., 2026b](#bib.bib7)), substitute a bounded hand-designed reward ([Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11)), or leave the metric raw and retune the exploration constant per setting ([Toledo et al., 2025](#bib.bib41)).\nKeeping the first term in \\([0,1]\\) also lets us adopt the exploration constant that the UCT analysis prescribes for bounded rewards, \\(c\\approx 1/\\sqrt{2}\\), without per-task tuning ([Kocsis & Szepesvári, 2006](#bib.bib22)).\nOur strategy for Best-of-N methods consists of running independent single Oneshot iterations, and then composing a set of bootstrapped-trajectories \\(B_{j}=\\{T_{1}^{j},\\ldots,T_{N}^{j}\\}\\) where each \\(T_{i}^{j}\\) is a composition of multiple independent Oneshot iterations of the requested time limit budget. Each trajectory is packed greedily so that the summed effective time of its iterations does not exceed the target budget, and an iteration that would overflow the trajectory is discarded rather than carried into the next one. The budget is therefore matched on the time the iterations actually consume, not on their nominal per-iteration time limit. We can then extract all metrics for each trajectory \\(T_{i}^{j}\\) by concatenating the submissions of all iterations in the trajectory. Finally, we sample multiple independent bootstrapped-trajectory sets \\(B_{1},\\ldots,B_{M}\\) and report the average-of-averages across this set.\nAppendix C Additional Chat, Oneshot and Malena experiments\nC.1 Oneshot time limit effect\nWe ablate the effect of the per-iteration time limit in our default Oneshot experimental setting for DeepSeek V4 Pro (Preview) and GLM 5.2 models.\nResults are in Figure [6](#A3.F6).\nWe observe, as expected, a monotonic improvement of per-iteration performance as the time budget increases.\nNonetheless, the actual performance of the aggregated Best-of-N composites quickly saturates at the three hour mark.\nIn our experimental setup, the six hour time limit default provides an reasonable choice to balance the performance and cost tradeoff.\nNote that, even when prompted to use the highest possible time budget of 12h, the agents still have a much lower average effective time usage, which is what allows us to aggregate Best-of-N at the 6h budget.\nC.2 Pairwise Oneshot search strategy and Malena agent comparison\n|  | Percentile (\\(\\uparrow\\)) | Medal rate (%, \\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | \n|---|---|---|---|\n|  | \\(\\Delta\\) | [1.409, 2.899] | \nC.3 Hidden validation gap\nIn Section [4](#S4) we observed that the gap of the performance between self-select (based on validation score) and oracle-best (based on actual test scores) becomes a problem, particularly for strategies that share less information between their iterations.\nIn this section, we analyze three different interventions that can help mitigate such issue.\nOur experiment setting is on the default Oneshot iteration configuration (six hour per-iteration time limit, GLM 5.2), and the baseline behavior control is the Best-of-N aggregation at 12h budget setting.\nWe compare:\n- V \nStrict validation guidelines: We inject to the prompt a set of strict validation guidelines the agent must adhere to, with the hopes to minimize data leakage.\n- BI \nBaseline iteration: At the beginning of the run, a Baseline iteration is spawned. The Baseline iteration aims to provide a simple, fast-to-run ML pipeline baseline to solve the task, while ensuring the validation split strategy follows a strict set of guidelines. Once this iteration ends, and until the 12h time limit, new Oneshot iterations use the Baseline’s workspace as starting point, while still remaining independent from each other. The goal for this intervention is to provide a common validation strategy for the iterations, and minimize the inconsistencies that come from differently designed sets.\n- HV \nHidden validation: At the beginning of the iteration, the harness prepares a deterministic validation-train split from the training data, using MLE-bench’s data preparation scripts. The agent does not have access to the target predictions of the hidden validation split. This strategy has the drawback of reducing the total amount of training data available to train the model in the first place. Note that, as described, this strategy cannot be deployed in practice, due to the hard requirement of the deterministic splitter which would require human intervention, and is therefore reported as a ceiling of what pre-splitting strategies can achieve.\n|  | Percentile (\\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | Selection gap (\\(\\downarrow\\)) | \n|---|---|---|---|\n|  | Self-select | Oracle |  | \n| BI | 61.24 [58.85, 63.82] | 63.26 [61.28, 65.38] | 2.024 [1.125, 2.901] | \n| base | 62.56 [60.34, 64.63] | 67.99 [66.74, 69.26] | 5.433 [3.681, 7.359] | \n| +V | 63.74 [59.93, 67.04] | 67.57 [66.23, 68.69] | 3.833 [0.936, 7.263] | \n| HV | 61.55 [59.73, 64.36] | 66.03 [63.38, 68.10] | 4.479 [2.412, 5.944] | \nTable [7](#A3.T7) shows the results of this experiment.\nWe see that the base independent Best-of-N Oneshot configuration provides the best percentile ceiling (oracle), while the introduction of the strict validation guidelines in the prompt alone allows for a slightly better self-select performance, at the cost of also lowering the oracle ceiling.\nFinally, the introduction of the initial baseline iteration provides the smallest gap of all interventions ablated, at the cost of a significantly worse performance ceiling.\nA plausible mechanism for these differences is the comparability of the validation scores themselves, rather than the number of submissions available to choose from: a single session produces all of its candidates under one validation split, so their reported scores are mutually comparable, whereas independent Best-of-N iterations each design their own split and the harness is then asked to rank scores that were never on a common scale.\nThe BI intervention is consistent with this reading, as it is the one that supplies a shared validation workspace.\nWe report this as a hypothesis and not as a result: it predicts that the gap should grow with the number of distinct validation methodologies in the pool rather than with the number of submissions, which we do not test here.\nWhile these results provide a quick overview about possible validation gap mitigation strategies, we acknowledge that at our current statistical power level, we cannot make strong conclusions about these results, and we encourage a more in-depth future work in this direction, including on the mechanism above.\nC.4 Malena budget scaling\nWe study how Malena scales under different time budgets.\nOur experimental setting consists of a DeepSeek V4 Pro (Preview) backbone, with a bare Malena iteration, meaning with the submission, system, and jobs tools disabled.\nThis serves as the minimal harness over a coding agent.\nIn our implementation, we use the submission tool to register the validation scores of each submission, and without it, we have no self-select mechanism.\nFor this reason, in this section we report only the oracle ceiling score which, despite not being a production-ready configuration, still gives a realistic range of the temporal behavior of the agent, especially since the Malena iteration shows the lowest validation gap in our tested iterations (see Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2)).\nFigure [7](#A3.F7) shows the results of Malena under different time limits.\nWe observe that the performance steadily increases as more budget is available.\nInterestingly, despite having an almost identical prompt where the only difference is the time limit mentioned itself, and the automatic time left reminders mentioned at the end of the turn,44\n              4\n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n              \n            Not to be confused with the Time nudge (N) intervention — which is also disabled in this experiment — which schedules automatic messages periodically regardless of the progress in the session turn. the performance of the agent at identical timeframes varies significantly depending on the total time budget allocated.\nThis suggests that the agent does adapt its exploration vs exploitation strategy depending on the time budget.\nC.5 Malena with different backbones\nWe test the performance of the Malena iteration with different backbone models, and compare it with the Best-of-N Oneshot composite at the 24h time budget target.\nOur selection of backbones is obtained from the results observed in Section [4.1](#S4.SS1), and we selected two frontier LLMs (GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3), both final releases of the DeepSeek V4 family, and Gemma 4 31B — a smaller model that still shows clear benefits from the agentic environment.\nWe plot our results in Figure [8](#A3.F8), and detailed paired-by-task comparison in Table [8](#A3.T8).\nWhile the frontier models show a clear benefit from the long single-session iteration, the percentile of the weaker model favors Best-of-N in comparison — although the medal rate remains consistently better in Malena iterations.\nThis may suggest that larger models can better leverage the autonomy in the single-session, or that the smaller context window of 256k in the weaker model becomes a bottleneck at this time scale, as some important details may be lost in the summarization during the context window compaction that is triggered when the context limit is reached.\nWe discuss this possibility further in Appendix [C.7](#A3.SS7).\n|  | Percentile (\\(\\uparrow\\)) | Medal rate (%, ablate the importance of all interventions of the Malena harness presented in Section [4.3](#S4.SS3), and we introduce a fourth intervention:\n- N \nTime nudges: Scheduled at 25%, 50%, 75% and 90%, the system automatically queues a brief message to the session and informs the agent about elapsed time. Note that for our default budget of 24h, the first time nudge is queued at 6h, which corresponds to the hard time limit of each Oneshot iteration.\nResults are shown in Table [9](#A3.T9), top row set.\nWe observe that for such long 24h sessions, the performance of the agent is negatively impacted when removing the system tool, suggesting that the agent struggles to track time correctly without it.\nOn the other hand, the addition of the time nudges results in a performance loss, perhaps due to a small distraction effect of unexpected messages. Finally, the removal of the jobs results in marginally better performance.\nFurthermore, we incorporate on top of the time nudges (N) intervention a wider set of general-purpose infrastructure enhancements, with the objective of better understanding where could the Malena iteration be aided by the external harness.\nNamely, we test: a resource nudge that reminds the agent when hardware utilization is low (R), the HuggingFace MCP that allows the agent to look for pretrained models (H), a set of strict validation guidelines in the prompt (V) and a set of eight skills covering common ML engineering strategies for iterating on a competition, from establishing baselines to diagnosing stagnation (s).\nMore information about these interventions in Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2).\nIn Table [9](#A3.T9), bottom row set, we show the results of this experiment.\nNone of the interventions provide significant performance improvements at our current statistical power.\nWe conclude that Malena as presented here serves as a capable enough baseline and further interventions either hurt performance, or do not produce significant improvements.\nThis is in contrast to Oneshot iterations, which may benefit from more complex harnesses as explored in Appendix [C.8](#A3.SS8).\nFinally, Table [10](#A3.T10) shows the pairwise comparison of all our ablations, including the ones presented in We compare the behavior on GLM 5.2 running at its native 1M context limit, with an artificially constrained version that at whenever the session hits the 64k context limit, it triggers OpenCode’s native session summarization compaction routine to reduce the context window.\n| Task | alaska2 | cassava | champs | freesound | hubmap | imet | kuzushiji | multi-modal | nfl | petfinder | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Avg | – | 1.2 | – | 1.5 | – | 2.0 | 4.2 | 12.5 | 2.5 | 1.7 | \nCompaction fires reliably under the shrunk budget. Every shrunk-context run triggers OpenCode’s built-in compaction at least once, at an average rate of 9.5 compactions over the 24h budget (it ranges from 3 to 63 compactions; over the \\(n=40\\) runs we observe 381 compaction events total). The context size right before compaction averages 65.4k tokens, tightly hugging the 64k threshold; the reset immediately after averages 15.6k tokens, i.e. each compaction discards roughly 76% of the accumulated context. Checking submission timing against compaction timing shows the run does not simply “beat” context pressure by locking in its medal before compaction kicks in: Table [11](#A3.T11) counts, per task, how many compactions occur before a run first reaches any medal. Across the 20/40 runs that achieve a medal, the model keeps making progress after 3.25 compactions on average (range 0–19) before its first medal, so repeated compaction cycles do not by themselves prevent the run from reaching a medal.\nPerformance drops under the shrunk budget.
