| 3/8/18 | \nAppendix D Additional experiments with external harnesses\nD.1 Detailed external harness description\nMLEvolve. ([Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib11)) MLEvolve maintains a tree of candidate solutions, each node a\ncomplete training script with a single parent, and expands it by UCT: nodes carry visit\ncounts and a backpropagated reward, and selection descends by the usual\nupper-confidence-bound rule with an exploration constant that decays over the run. The\nbackpropagated reward is not the task metric but a small hand-designed signal — a bonus\nfor beating the incumbent, a penalty for a node that fails to run. Past the halfway point\nof the budget the selector blends UCT with rank-weighted sampling from a global,\ngreedily-ranked top-\\(K\\), so exploration gives way to exploitation on a schedule rather than\nthrough the search rule itself. Six operators produce nodes: draft,\nimprove, evolution (improve using the branch’s own trajectory),\ndebug, and two cross-branch operators, fusion and fusion_draft,\nwhich synthesize a candidate from the best nodes of several branches. Crossover therefore\nflows through the prompt rather than the topology: a fusion node still has exactly\none parent, with the other candidates injected as reference text, so the structure is a tree\nwith cross-branch information flow and not a genealogical graph. Three candidates execute\nconcurrently on one GPU. It commits, greedily and with no end-of-run re-ranking, whichever\nsubmission had the best self-reported validation metric at the time it was produced — a\nnumber an LLM extracts from the candidate’s own stdout. It also keeps a hybrid BM25+FAISS\nretrieval memory over past attempts within a run, indexing each successful node by its plan\nand a code summary (and each debug node by the error it was repairing) and querying it when\ndrafting, improving and debugging; we enable it in all runs reported here.\nAiScientist. ([Chen et al., 2026a](#bib.bib6)) AiScientist has no search structure: it is a single conversation\nstepping through tool calls, and what to do next is whatever tool the model names. State\npersists in one mutable git repository plus two append-only markdown logs, one for\nimplementation and one for experiments; whenever the agent implements or runs an experiment,\nthe harness greps the most recent block of the other log and prepends it. This\n“file-as-bus” is the whole of its memory — there is no retrieval index, and the only\nother summarization fires on context overflow. Its operators are analyze_data,\nprioritize_tasks, implement (whose modes cover full/fix/explore/refine/ensemble, so debugging is a mode rather than a separate operator), run_experiment,\nspawn_subagent and submit, alongside shell primitives. Work is strictly\nsequential; subagents run blocking, and the only concurrency is whatever background training\nthe model chooses to launch inside its sandbox. Most consequentially for\nSection [5.1](#S5.SS1), the final choice among candidates is made by the agent rather\nthan by harness code: no code ranks candidates, but the agent is required, as a step before\nsubmitting, to compare its candidates against the registry and its experiment log and copy\nthe best to the canonical submission path. It does so substantively — where it records the\ndecision, it cites the cross-validation or hold-out protocol it designed, per-candidate\nscores under that protocol, and a rationale comparing them (for instance choosing a blended\nensemble at OOF \\(F_{1}\\) \\(0.944\\) over its two components at \\(0.942\\) and \\(0.938\\)). Its\nself-selected number is therefore a genuine self-selection on its own validation signal, as\nfor the other methods; the difference is that the mechanism is prompt-driven rather than\nenforced, and the structured record of it is present in only a minority of runs.\nArbor. ([Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib20)) A research agent built around a Coordinator/Executor loop over a\npersistent “Idea Tree”: the Coordinator maintains the tree of candidate ideas and their\noutcomes, the Executor implements and evaluates a chosen idea in an isolated git worktree\nper experiment, and the loop expands whichever branch of the tree looks most promising.\nTwo run modes exist upstream: a native CLI over a swappable backbone LLM, and a\nkeyless coding-agent skill-suite mode where the host coding agent’s own model does the\nreasoning; this paper uses the native-CLI mode.\nScienceFlow. ([Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib52)) A config/manifest-driven long-horizon ML-research agent\n(scienceflow.cli, verbs repl/parallel/monitor): a run\nis specified as a YAML task manifest and executed by one or more parallel exploratory\nworkers under a chosen concurrency level, orchestrating both the agent’s own reasoning\ncalls and the ML training/evaluation code the workers produce.\nD.2 Detailed MLE-bench results\nIn Table [15](#A4.T15), we provide more details on the headline results presented in Figure [1](#S1.F1) of Malena, along with the different external harnesses we ran, on the fixed30 split in MLE-bench.\nAdditionally, Table [16](#A4.T16) shows the paired-by-task relative performance for Malena against all other methods.\nWe find that Malena outperforms every presented baseline at the GLM 5.2 backbone across both performance metrics; obtains strictly better percentile scores for Kimi K3, and either beats or comes up as statistically indistinguishable at the DeepSeek V4 Flash.\nThe only backbone where Malena presents a significant regression against other baselines is with Gemma 4 31B — the weaker of all models tested — where MLEvolve outperforms according to the percentile metric, while maintaining an equivalent medal rate at our current statistical power.\n|  | Percentile (\\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | Medal rate (\\(\\uparrow\\)) |  | \n|---|---|---|---|---|\n|  | -6.36]* | -4.4 [-10.0, +1.1] | 4/6/20 | \nTwo data caveats apply across methods. First, a run with no valid submission is\nimputed at the worst possible percentile rather than dropped (Section [5.1](#S5.SS1)’s\nconvention); this creates exact ties between two very different failure modes and gives a\nhandful of large-magnitude gaps outsized leverage on every method’s point estimate or test\nstatistic. Second, seed counts per task can be uneven, which the per-task-mean-based\nmethods treat as equally reliable regardless of count.\nD.3 Tuning effort invested in baseline harnesses\nBecause Malena is our own system, a natural concern is that we invested asymmetrically\nmore integration and tuning effort into it than into the external harnesses it is compared\nagainst. This appendix reports that effort, spanning backbone-compatibility bug fixes and\ntargeted configuration ablations on MLEvolve, ScienceFlow, and Arbor. In each case, this\nwork either (i) fixed a genuine defect — adopted into every canonical batch reported\nelsewhere in the paper; (ii) found no reliable improvement, in which case we kept the\nharness’s original canonical configuration and report the negative result here rather than\nomitting it; or (iii) found a genuine backbone-specific configuration effect and adopted the\nbetter setting as canonical — the one such case is MLEvolve’s reasoning effort on\nDeepSeek V4 Flash (Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)).\nMLEvolve: reasoning effort on DeepSeek V4 Flash.\nFor every other backbone reported above, MLEvolve’s canonical batches use that backbone’s own reasoning_effort=max (or equivalent) setting, matching every other method’s convention. On DeepSeek V4 Flash, we instead adopt reasoning_effort=medium as MLEvolve’s canonical setting, the only backbone where it departs from max. The reason is a length effect specific to this backbone: at max, DeepSeek V4 Flash emits substantially longer reasoning traces than it does at medium. Because MLEvolve’s tree search issues many independent, short LLM calls rather than growing a single session, per-call reasoning length translates almost directly into wall-clock cost per search step, so under max’s much longer traces MLEvolve completes only a fraction of the search steps it completes at medium within a fixed 24h budget.\nTo quantify this we compare max and medium on an identical 10-task subset\n(same tasks, same seed range, same 24h timeout for both arms; Table [17](#A4.T17)).\nCutting reasoning effort to medium reduces the median reasoning length per LLM call\nby roughly 5\\(\\times\\) (37,124 to 7,069 characters), which more than triples the mean number\nof completed search-tree nodes per run (18.5 to 62.8). Per-node quality is essentially\nunchanged between the two settings — the buggy-node rate is within noise (61% vs. 62%)\n— so the additional search volume at medium is not, on average, lower-quality than\nmax’s, just far more numerous. That additional search volume alone is enough to move\noutcomes substantially: any-medal rate rises from 0% to 25.0% (95% CI [10.0, 30.0]) and mean\npercentile from 42.6 (95% CI [36.0, 48.5]) to 53.1 (95% CI [44.9, 59.8]).\n| reasoning_effort | Any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | None-rate (%) \\(\\downarrow\\) | Mean percentile \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Median reasoning (chars) \\(\\downarrow\\) | Mean nodes/run \\(\\uparrow\\) | Buggy rate (%) \\(\\downarrow\\) | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| max | 0.0 [0.0, 0.0] | 13.8 | 42.6 [36.0, 48.5] | 37124 | 18.5 | 61 | \n| medium (canonical) | 25.0 [10.0, 30.0] | 2.5 | 53.1 [44.9, 59.8] | 7069 | 62.8 | 62 | \nIndependent tool-calling failures on GLM 5.2.\nBefore either harness’s canonical GLM 5.2 batch could run, we found and fixed two unrelated bugs that both manifested as tool-calling failures specific to this backbone. MLEvolve’s structured-output calls request a named (forced) tool_choice; GLM 5.2 served behind our vLLM deployment enters grammar-constrained decoding under a forced choice, and its FSM has no transition for the reasoning tokens the model wants to emit before its answer, so every such call failed with an internal server error. We fixed this by defaulting MLEvolve’s structured-output calls to tool_choice=auto (configurable per stage, so backends that do handle a forced choice reliably are unaffected). Independently, ScienceFlow’s LLM client always included a tools/tool_choice field in its request payload, even for calls with no tools to offer (e.g., its metric-output-interpreter step); the same GLM 5.2 deployment rejects an explicit empty tools: [] array outright, which we fixed by omitting the field entirely rather than sending an empty array. These are two independent root causes, each surfaced by validating against a live endpoint before launching the canonical batch, not two symptoms of the same underlying fix.\nScienceFlow: worker count and resource-admission policy.\nScienceFlow’s canonical configuration runs multiple parallel exploratory workers sharing a single GPU (2 workers for GLM 5.2; a single worker gated by an LLM-based resource-admission heuristic for DeepSeek V4 Flash). Suspecting that this concurrency could itself be a source of resource congestion, we tested two independent mitigations, one per backbone.\nOn GLM 5.2, we compared 1 worker against the canonical 2 workers across the full\nfixed30 split (Table [18](#A4.T18)). Any-medal\nrate and mean percentile are statistically indistinguishable between the two worker counts;\nthe 1-worker point estimate is nominally higher on any-medal rate (43.9% vs. 40.0%). This\ncomparison uses the full fixed30 split, following an inconclusive result on an earlier\n11-task subset; CIs at this sample size cannot rule out a modest true effect. We kept the\ncanonical 2-worker configuration.\nOn DeepSeek V4 Flash, we replaced ScienceFlow’s canonical resource_smart_llm\nadmission mode — an LLM-based admission heuristic plus a main-agent advisory signal that\ndecides when a worker may start a new GPU job — with the static rule-based\nresource_smart_policy, on the same canonical 10-task ablation subset used by\nAppendix [D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)’s MLEvolve ablation, to check whether the added\ncomplexity of the canonical policy was necessary (Table [19](#A4.T19)). It\nwas not: any-medal rate and mean percentile are both somewhat lower under\nresource_smart_policy, and the gap is within the noise we see elsewhere at this sample size. We kept\nresource_smart_llm as canonical.\n[D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)’s MLEvolve ablation. Both any-medal and mean percentile are oracle (best-of-pool) across every candidate ScienceFlow harvests per run, flipped so higher is better, matching every other percentile column in this paper. 95% CIs are bootstrapped over per-task run lists (2000 resamples), matching the convention used for the paper’s headline tables (Section\n[5.1](#S5.SS1)).\n| Configuration | Any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Gold (%) | Silver (%) | Bronze (%) | Mean percentile \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| resource_smart_llm (canonical) | 28.3 [10.0, 50.0] | 10.0 | 13.3 | 5.0 | 66.6 [52.9, 80.7] | \n| resource_smart_policy | 20.0 [0.0, 40.0] | 2.5 | 10.0 | 7.5 | 52.2 [39.6, 65.5] | \nArbor: executor timeout and convergence early-stopping (GLM 5.2).\nArbor’s canonical\nconfiguration for both GLM 5.2 and DeepSeek V4 Flash uses reasoning_effort=max, a\n1M-token context window, 3 parallel executors, and a 4-hour timeout per individual experiment\ninside its 24h wall-clock budget; the executor timeout and the stop_after=8\nconvergence early-stopping threshold ablated below are not values we chose — both are\nported unmodified from the mle_kaggle competition-optimization plugin bundled in\nArbor’s own public GitHub repository, so these ablations test Arbor’s own maintainers’\ndefaults rather than a configuration we picked ourselves. We ran two targeted ablations\nagainst this configuration. First, we tripled the executor timeout to\n12 hours; the medal rate is\nunchanged (Table [20](#A4.T20)). Second, we tried relaxing\nArbor’s convergence-based early stopping (default stop_after=8 consecutive\nnon-improving idea-tree expansions) to stop_after=999, effectively disabling it so a\nrun keeps exploring until its wall-clock budget runs out rather than stopping early: medal rate\nis again unchanged within noise\n(Table [20](#A4.T20)).\n| Ablation | Baseline any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Ablation any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | \n|---|---|---|\n| Executor timeout: 4h \\(\\to\\) 12h | 12.5 [0.0, 50.0] | 12.5 [0.0, 50.0] | \n| Convergence early-stop: stop_after 8 \\(\\to\\) 999 | 50.0 [0.0, 100.0] | 43.8 [25.0, 75.0] | \nD.4 Trajectory and token usage with Kimi K3\nFigure [3](#S5.F3)’s two panels are GLM 5.2 only. Figure [9](#A4.F9) reproduces\nboth at Kimi K3 using the same fixed30 task set.\nThe overall shape matches Figure [3](#S5.F3): quality rises quickly in the\nfirst few hours and plateaus thereafter, with AiScientist again the slowest to converge. On\ncost, Malena’s cache-read volume again dominates its token footprint the same way it does\nat GLM 5.2, but Kimi K3’s much higher output pricing ($13.99/M vs. GLM 5.2’s $3.123/M)\nmakes MLEvolve’s uncached, output-heavy tree search the most expensive of the four shown\nhere.\nD.5 Quality by task difficulty\nIn order to better understand the strengths of Malena against other baselines, and better investigate its improved performance, we partition the fixed30 split into four different clusters, according to perceived difficulty among all harnesses:\n- 1. \nnomad2018, plant, spooky, stanford, us\n- 2. \naptos2019, freesound, h&m, hotel, hubmap, mlsp, tweet, whale\n- 3. \nbillion-word, cassava, imet, kuzushiji, multi-modal, nfl, petfinder\n- 4. \nalaska2, champs, hms, jigsaw, new, osic, smartphone, tensorflow2, uw, ventilator\nWe plot the individual oracle performance among these clusters in Figure [10](#A4.F10).\nOn the easiest cluster (1), all methods approach ceiling performance by 24h, though AiScientist lags behind for much of the run before catching up.\nOn cluster 2, Malena and MLEvolve maintain a sustained edge over Arbor and AiScientist through 24h.\nMalena performs significantly better than all other baselines on cluster 3, its clearest lead of the four clusters, suggesting a sweet spot between tasks that are inherently difficult for current AI agents and tasks that are comparatively easy.\nOn the hardest cluster (4), Malena keeps a smaller but consistent edge over the other three, which track closely together.\nD.6 NatureBench: evaluation protocol\nFor NatureBench, we follow the benchmark’s own online evaluation protocol as published\n(Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3)): rather than a single held-out grader run once at the end\nof a run, as in MLE-bench, each task exposes a live /evaluate HTTP endpoint that an\nagent may query an unbounded number of times over the course of a run, with the endpoint\nautomatically tracking the best score seen across all calls. We give every harness we\ncompare here — Malena, AiScientist, and MLEvolve — unrestricted access to this\nendpoint, matching NatureBench’s protocol as published.\nWe extend the per-run time budget to 8h, double NatureBench’s own published default of 4h, to give the slower of our search-based baselines (MLEvolve, whose tree search advances through many short, sequential steps rather than one long session) a more comfortable margin within which to complete meaningful search.\nBecause the harness’s own protocol reports the best score achieved anywhere in the run —\nan oracle score, as described in Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3) — rather than a\nself-selected final submission, this metric implicitly rewards however many independent\nattempts a harness manages to get graded within the time budget, on top of whatever quality\ngains its search process makes. Table [21](#A4.T21) reports the number of\n/evaluate calls per run for each harness, across our full NatureBench run cohort.\nMalena and AiScientist call the endpoint at a broadly similar rate\n(median 16 and 18 calls per run, respectively); MLEvolve calls it substantially less often\n(median 8 calls per run). Since a benefit under an oracle-best-of-run metric scales with the\nnumber of independent scoring opportunities exercised, this asymmetry may put MLEvolve at a\nmechanical disadvantage on this particular metric, independent of any difference in the\nquality of the strategies it discovers.\n|  | Malena | AiScientist | MLEvolve | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| p25 | 8 | 7 | 2 | \n| p50 (median) | 16 | 18 | 8 | \n| p75 | 36 | 96 | 18 | \nAppendix E Trace Analysis\nThis appendix gives the full methodology behind Section [5.2](#S5.SS2)’s checkpoint\nlabeling and the technique-overlap analysis it enables, including the labeling\nschema, the offline deduplication/clustering procedure, and technique-name\nnormalization we omitted from the main text for space. Labeling was run on a fixed set of\n10 original MLE-bench tasks (aptos2019, billion-word-imputation,\ncassava, champs, freesound, h&m, jigsaw,\nnfl, petfinder, ventilator) – not to be confused with the\nfixed10 challenging split used elsewhere in this paper, which is a different\nset of 10 tasks. The checkpoint-mining and\nlabeling steps below (through canonicalization) are the current pipeline behind\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) and\nFigure [4](#S5.F4). Unless stated otherwise, the\nlabeling/judge model throughout is GLM 5.2.\nE.1 Checkpoint mining\nCheckpoints. We unify run traces across methods to a common checkpoint format: a snapshot of the run’s code repository at specific points in time, method-dependent since each harness structures its own trajectory differently – for Malena, immediately before a long job submission, a long bash tool call, or a submission-registration tool call; for MLEvolve, before executing a candidate; for AiScientist, before a long bash tool call; for Arbor, before a job-submit call. Oneshot and Completions have no intermediate trajectory to snapshot and are labeled once, on the final state of the repository. Runs with multiple checkpoints are labeled sequentially along this chain, each job seeded with the entries already logged for its nearest earlier checkpoint so it only has to add, remove, or revise entries rather than re-deriving the whole technique set from scratch.\nLabeling schema (pass 1: technique extraction). The judge (GLM 5.2) reads a checkpoint’s code and logs zero or more entries, each with: a free-form snake_case name (no reuse-search against prior entries – naming convergence is handled later, offline); 1–3 categories, ordered most-to-least applicable, drawn from a fixed 13-way taxonomy (data_preprocessing, data_pipeline, feature_engineering, augmentation, architecture, loss, regularization, optimization, training, ensembling, model_selection, post_processing, inference, plus an other escape hatch (used for only 0.02% of all logged category-tag annotations across our labeled checkpoints), which we exclude from the category-share figures throughout this section); and three separate text fields instead of one blob – mechanism (task-agnostic, roughly 40 tokens, describing only WHAT IS COMPUTED, stripped of domain nouns and hyperparameters, the only field ever embedded for similarity/dedup), instantiation (task-specific detail, hyperparameters, and domain nouns, kept for search but never embedded), and evidence_sig (a 1–3 line canonical code excerpt for human/reviewer sanity-checking). Splitting mechanism from instantiation this way removes a specific noise source found in an earlier embedding-similarity audit, where chunk embeddings picked up code-context co-occurrence (e.g. dropout/mlp/layer_norm clustering together purely because they appear near each other in a forward pass) rather than technique identity.\nDeduplication and canonicalization (offline, per task). All identity resolution – deciding whether two independently-logged entries describe the same underlying technique – happens offline, never at labeling time, and entirely within one task’s own runs (never pooled across tasks, so no task’s vocabulary is built from or reviewed against another task’s runs). Pass-1 entries are embedded on their mechanism text using Qwen3-Embedding-8B and grouped by complete-linkage hierarchical clustering at a fixed cosine-similarity threshold (0.70, picked by sweeping method \\(\\times\\) threshold against a hand-audited corpus for cluster-size and same-run-duplicate violations). An automated GLM 5.2 review then runs in two phases: a split-review phase flags multi-member clusters with no category shared by every member and asks GLM 5.2 whether any member should be extracted into its own cluster; a merge-review phase takes every singleton cluster and asks the judge whether it is really the same technique as one of its nearest neighbors by mechanism-embedding similarity. A final canonicalization pass has GLM 5.2 synthesize one canonical name and mechanism description per cluster that a reader would recognize as covering every member’s variant, rather than mechanically picking one member’s text. Pass 2 then re-labels every run a second time, this time against the finalized, canonical cluster set: the judge calls log_known_technique(cluster_id, ...) directly when a checkpoint’s technique matches an existing cluster, and free-form entries otherwise, so a pass-2 match is never subject to a separate, error-prone post-hoc nearest-centroid assignment step.\nE.2 Main-text plot methodology\nThis subsection gives the exact construction behind the two main-text plots built on top\nof the canonical technique clusters above: the category-share-decay curves\n(Figure [5](#S5.F5)) and the per-technique rarity distribution\n(Figure [4](#S5.F4)).\nCategory-share decay (half-life). Figure [5](#S5.F5)\ntracks a method’s technique-category composition as a function of checkpoint\nfraction \\(x\\in[0,1]\\) (progress through a run, pooled over every checkpoint of every run\nof that method). Every technique discovered at fraction \\(f\\leq x\\) contributes weight\n\\(0.5^{(x-f)/h}\\) to \\(x\\)’s composition, for half-life \\(h\\): a technique discovered exactly at\n\\(x\\) has full weight 1, one discovered \\(h\\) earlier has weight 0.5, and older ones decay\nsmoothly toward (but never reach) 0. Weighted category-tag counts are normalized to shares\nat each \\(x\\). \\(h\\to\\infty\\) recovers a plain cumulative count of every technique discovered\nso far; \\(h\\to 0\\) recovers an immediate, local snapshot of only the most recently discovered\ntechniques. The main text uses \\(h=0.25\\); Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3)\nbelow checks smaller half-lives and per-task (non-pooled) curves.\nTechnique rarity.\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) reports, per method,\nthe distribution of how rare the canonical technique clusters that method’s runs exhibit\nare. Rarity is computed independently per task, not globally: for a fixed task, pool\nevery run of every (method, backbone) group on that task, and for each cluster \\(c\\) let \\(n_{c}\\)\nbe how many of those pooled runs exhibit it; \\(c\\)’s rarity is \\(1-n_{c}/n_{\\text{total}}\\), the\nfraction of that task’s pooled runs that did not use it. Pooling within the task\nacross every group (rather than, say, comparing a group only to one fixed baseline) means\nrarity reflects how many runs overall exhibit a technique, not how many distinct\ngroups do – a technique used by only a handful of runs stays rare even when those runs\nhappen to span more than one group (Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5) gives a\nrarity-0.9 example shared across exactly two groups).\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) plots, per method, the resulting distribution of\nthese per-technique rarity values (pooled across backbones and 10 tasks) into\nsix windows centered at rarity \\(0,0.2,\\ldots,1.0\\) (half-width \\(0.1\\)).\nE.3 Robustness of the category-share-decay curves\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) in the main text pools all\nlabeled tasks at a single half-life (0.25). Figure [11](#A5.F11)\nshows the same pooled construction at three half-lives closer to the main\ntext’s value (0.1, 0.15, 0.2), and\nFigures [12](#A5.F12) and [13](#A5.F13)\nshow the identical three-half-life construction on two individual tasks\n(aptos2019 and nfl)\ninstead of pooled, to check that the shape of Malena’s late-run shift toward\nensembling/model_selection is not an artifact of the particular half-life or\nof pooling across tasks.\nE.4 Per-task technique-category composition across methods\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) and\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) both look at a method’s technique\nchoices pooled across all 10 tasks – respectively how its category mix\nshifts over the course of a run, and how rare its techniques are relative to\nevery other group. A complementary question is whether methods reach for a\ndifferent mix of technique categories than each other on the\nsame task, i.e. whether task identity or method identity is the\nbigger driver of what a method tries. Here we look directly at the\ntechnique-category composition of each method’s repertoire on each task\nindependently.\nFor a given (task, method) pair, we take the set of every distinct canonical\ntechnique cluster (Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1) above) any of that\nmethod’s backbone/seed runs on that task exhibit, and tally, for each\ncategory tag in the taxonomy above, how many of those clusters carry it (a\ncluster tagged with 2 categories contributes to both – categories are not\nsplit fractionally, so the resulting bar shows a share of category-tags,\nnot of techniques). This is a presence-based snapshot (did the method ever\ntry a category-tagged technique at all over the course of the run), not\nusage-frequency-weighted like Figure [5](#S5.F5)’s\ndecay curves – deliberately simpler, since here we only need one static\ncomposition profile per (task, method) rather than a time series.\nFigure [14](#A5.F14) shows the resulting composition\nfor all 10 tasks. Two things stand out. First, task identity is a strong\ndriver on its own: feature_engineering dominates every method’s\nprofile on champs,\nh&m, and\nnfl, while ensembling dominates on\npetfinder and, for Malena and AiScientist\nspecifically, on aptos2019,\ncassava, and\njigsaw as well. Second,\non top of that shared task-driven baseline, Malena and AiScientist’s\nprofiles track each other closely on most tasks (e.g. aptos2019,\ncassava,\nfreesound,\nnfl), while MLEvolve is the most frequent\noutlier – visibly heavier on data_preprocessing and lighter on\nensembling on several of those same tasks, consistent with its\npooled under-representation of ensembling growth in\nFigure [5](#S5.F5).\nThe task jigsaw shows the largest\ncross-method spread: Oneshots’ profile there is missing most of the\nensembling share the other three methods have, replaced by a much\nlarger loss/optimization block.\nE.5 Illustrative session excerpts\nThe excerpts below are verbatim (only trimmed for length, marked with ...) pulls\nfrom real session transcripts, given as illustrative examples of the reasoning/tool-call\nstructure behind the checkpoint-labeling claims in Section [5.2](#S5.SS2). Insert new_whale based on threshold... Use validation set to find optimal threshold. </reasoning>\nStage 1 (src/train.py) trains a CNN/ViT backbone with an ArcFace loss to produce normalized whale-fluke embeddings. Stage 2 (src/retrieve.py, src/centroid_submit.py, src/detector.py) computes cosine-similarity nearest neighbors against the training embeddings, ranks top-5 candidates via per-class centroids, and uses a small logistic-regression classifier over similarity-gap features (top-1/2/3 similarity, gaps, top-5 mean/std) to flag new_whale queries – individuals never seen in training. All 63 subsequent checkpoints are targeted patches to this fixed pipeline rather than new stages: backbone swaps (EfficientNetV2 \\(\\to\\) ConvNeXt \\(\\to\\) Swin), input-resolution bumps, novelty-threshold retuning, and ensembling. A better new-detector could use... but let me first improve the embedding... </reasoning>\nThat is, the agent is choosing which fixed sub-module of the pipeline to improve next, at the granularity of a targeted code change, rather than tuning a numeric hyperparameter or introducing a new pipeline stage.\nExample 5: independent convergence on soft-label self-training,\naptos2019 (GLM 5.2 vs. Kimi K3).\nMalena’s GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3 runs on aptos2019\nindependently converge on the same canonical technique cluster – soft-label\nself-training (pseudo-labeling using an ensemble’s own predictions as soft\ntargets) – without sharing a run, then diverge in how much surrounding\nmachinery each backbone builds around it. This cluster has rarity 0.9\n(Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2)): across every AiScientist, MLEvolve, and\nOneshot run on this task, none use it.\nThe GLM 5.2 run (logged as pseudo_label_self_training):\nMechanism: Generate soft pseudo-labels by running a trained model on unlabeled data, combine the pseudo-labeled samples with the original labeled training set (using soft target distributions and a per-sample weight for pseudo-labeled examples), and retrain the model on the augmented dataset. Instantiation: dataset.py PseudoDataset combines train images (hard labels, soft-onehot with label smoothing) and pseudo-labeled test images (soft probs from ensemble predictions). train_pl.py orchestrates per-fold pseudo-label training. Pseudo-labels sourced from infer.py --save_test_probs output.\nThe Kimi K3 run (logged independently as self_training_pseudo_labels), landing on the identical mechanism:\nInstantiation: train_pseudo.py loads ensemble test probability predictions (from compute_ensemble_test_probs.py) as soft pseudo-labels for unlabeled test images. Trains jointly on labeled train data (one-hot hard targets) and pseudo-labeled test data (soft 5-class probs) via soft cross-entropy... Pseudo data influence controlled by --pseudo_repeat (dataset repetition factor)... Supports per-fold warm-start via --init_from_dir. Evidence: comb = ConcatDataset([train_ds_hard, *([train_ds_pseudo] * cfg.pseudo_repeat)]) loss = -(targets * logp) if weight is not None: loss = loss * weight[None, :].to(device)\nThe two runs share the core mechanism but not its surroundings. Before\nbeing folded into the single canonical cluster above during the offline canonicalization\nstage, the GLM 5.2 run builds substantially more machinery around it: a\nweighted soft-cross-entropy loss (pseudo-labeled samples down-weighted 0.2\nvs. 1.0 for labeled ones), pseudo-label sharpening (raising soft\nprobabilities to a power \\(>1\\) and renormalizing to peak the target\ndistribution), iterative multi-round pseudo-labeling (retraining on a\nprevious round’s ensemble output, then keeping whichever round scored better\nper model on OOF), and two rounds of brute-force combinatorial search –\nfirst over which round/resolution each ensemble member should use (up to 324\ncombinations, each scored by out-of-fold threshold-optimized QWK), then over\n7 candidate final ensemble configurations. The Kimi K3 run instead builds a\nleaner version of the same core loop, adding per-fold warm-starting\n(--init_from_dir) rather than the GLM 5.2 run’s iterative-round\nsearch. Both runs independently rediscover the same underlying technique –\nreflected in Figure [4](#S5.F4) as a rare (0.9) but\nrecurring point in Malena’s rarity distribution – while differing in how\nmuch extra engineering each backbone invests around it.\nAppendix F Contamination check\nEvery fixed30 task is a public Kaggle competition, and MLE-bench itself ships a set of reference kernels/notebooks for a subset of its competitions (public solutions collected as part of the benchmark’s own construction). We check for memorized/copied code using a vendored copy of Dolos, MLE-bench’s own source-code similarity tool, against a curated corpus of 1,350 distinct real solutions spanning all 30 fixed30 tasks.\nCode similarity (Dolos). Dolos’s default pairwise similarity score\nnormalizes shared token \\(k\\)-grams by the union of both files’ tokens (a Jaccard-style\nmeasure), which is a poor fit for our setting and, notably, biased in the direction of\nunder-reporting contamination rather than over-reporting it: Malena’s solution\nfiles are typically much smaller than the monolithic Kaggle notebooks in the real corpus,\nso even if Malena fully reused a small fragment of a large real file verbatim, the\nshared tokens would be a small fraction of the (large) union and the similarity score\nwould stay low – the metric would fail to flag exactly the kind of partial copying we\ncare about. We instead score every pair by containment – the fraction of\nthe smaller-file’s tokens that also appear in the other file, together with the\nlongest matching token run – which asks “how much of this file was drawn from the\nother”, independent of how large the other file is, and is sensitive to exactly the\nsmall-fragment-fully-reused case that raw similarity would miss. We compare\nMalena-vs-real containment against a real-vs-real cross-label null baseline (distinct,\nindependently-written real solutions, pooled across the whole 30-task corpus rather than\nrestricted to same-task pairs – see below for why). Table [22](#A6.T22)\nreports the containment distribution for both, across all 924,750 Malena-vs-real pairs\nand 473,850 real-vs-real cross-label pairs (every Malena checkpoint / every real\nsolution, pooled across all 30 tasks – i.e. including task-mismatched pairs, not just\nsame-task ones – exhaustive): Malena-vs-real containment sits below real-vs-real\ncontainment at every percentile shown. A pair is flagged only if it exceeds both\nthe real-vs-real containment and longest-match-run 95th percentiles;\nzero of the 924,750 pairs are flagged. This is the pattern we would expect\nfrom independently-written code, not from copied or memorized solutions. (The embedding\ncheck below restricts to same-task pairs only, to keep the \\(O(n^{2})\\) embedding-similarity\ncost tractable; the two pair counts are therefore not directly comparable.)\nEmbedding similarity (bag-of-chunks). As a second, token-independent check, we\nembed both corpora with Qwen3-Embedding-8B ([Zhang et al., 2025](#bib.bib49)), which is a 4096-dim embedding model, splitting each file into a sliding\nwindow of chunks (since many solution files exceed the embedding model’s context window)\nand representing each solution as a bag of chunk embeddings rather than a single vector.\nWe compare bags with three metrics: mean-pooling cosine (cosine similarity of the\ntwo bags’ mean embeddings); Chamfer similarity (for each chunk in one bag, its max\ncosine similarity to any chunk in the other bag, averaged over the bag, symmetrized by\naveraging both directions); and Chamfer with background subtraction (the raw\nChamfer score minus a background level – the median Chamfer score over 5,000 random\nMalena-vs-real chunk-bag pairs drawn from different tasks, 0.504 – to remove the\ngeneric code/library-idiom similarity any two unrelated files share). We compute all three\nover every same-task Malena-vs-real pair (62,207 pairs) and every same-task\nreal-vs-real cross-label pair (33,465 pairs), exhaustively, across all 30 tasks.\nTable [22](#A6.T22) reports the percentiles alongside the Dolos\nnumbers above. At the median (and, for mean-pooling cosine, up to the 90th percentile),\nMalena-vs-real similarity is comparable to the real-vs-real baseline rather than below\nit; but at the percentiles where copied or memorized content would actually show up –\nthe upper tail, p95 and p99 – Malena-vs-real similarity is consistently below\nthe real-vs-real baseline for all three metrics, i.e. there is no anomalously heavy right\ntail. This is the same qualitative conclusion as the Dolos check, from an independent,\ntoken-free signal.\n| metric | group | p50 | p90 | p95 | p99 | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Dolos containment | real-vs-real | 0.008 | 0.077 | 0.143 | 0.453 | \n| Dolos containment | Malena-vs-real | 0.002 | 0.009 | 0.013 | 0.020 | \n| mean-pooling cosine | real-vs-real | 0.735 | 0.877 | 0.909 | 0.968 | \n| mean-pooling cosine | Malena-vs-real | 0.773 | 0.880 | 0.896 | 0.919 | \n| Chamfer | real-vs-real | 0.634 | 0.741 | 0.778 | 0.882 | \n| Chamfer | Malena-vs-real | 0.638 | 0.713 | 0.726 | 0.746 | \n| Chamfer (bg-subtracted) | real-vs-real | 0.130 | 0.237 | 0.273 | 0.377 | \n| Chamfer (bg-subtracted) | Malena-vs-real | 0.134 | 0.209 | 0.222 | 0.242 | \nBehavioral check: environment interaction matters. A separate, model-independent\nargument against memorization comes from comparing iteration types rather than code\ndirectly: if Malena’s solutions were largely memorized from pretraining, a single\ncompletion asked to produce a full solution to a competition it has “seen” should score\nclose to the full agentic loop, since no environment feedback would be needed to recall a\nmemorized answer.\nWe can clearly observe that such thing is not the case in our corpus.\nFrom Figure [2](#S4.F2), we can observe that even Best-of-N Oneshot iterations aggregated at 6h time budgets performs significantly better than the single Chat iteration.\nThis is the opposite of what contamination would predict, and is consistent with the agent doing real iterative\nproblem-solving rather than recalling known answers.\nAppendix G Discussion\nG.1 Limitations\nThe primary limitation of this study is that most of our results rely on\nMLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), which has known issues in its\ntask preparation scripts and, being built from public Kaggle competitions, is a plausible\ntarget for pretraining contamination. We mitigate this in three ways. First, we identified\nand fixed the subset of fixed30 tasks with broken preparation scripts (missing data\nor ground-truth leakage) rather than excluding them, so that our comparisons stay on the\nfull 30-task set used elsewhere in the paper; see Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1) and\nTable [3](#A1.T3) for which tasks were fixed. Second, we directly checked for contamination via\ntwo independent methods, a code-similarity (Dolos) check and a bag-of-embeddings\nsimilarity check, both against a curated corpus of real fixed30 solutions and both finding\nno anomalous similarity between Malena-generated code and real solutions (Appendix [F](#A6)).\nThird, we additionally evaluate on NatureBench ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib43))\n(Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3)), whose tasks are recent open scientific-research questions\nwith no public solution corpus to memorize, though there we could only afford a smaller\nnumber of models and fewer methods than on fixed30.\nA second limitation is statistical power. Some of our results still have confidence\nintervals wide enough that we cannot make strong claims about the size of an effect; we\nbelieve our weaker claims of Malena being no worse than a given baseline are well\nsupported (Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5) reports the paired-comparison methodology this\nrelies on), but stronger claims about exactly how much better should be read with this in\nmind. This is a direct consequence of cost: running agents for the full 24-hour budget is\nexpensive, so we could only afford 3–4 seeds per task at 24 hours, versus 6–10 seeds per\ntask for our 6-hour and completion-only conditions.\nA third limitation is scope on coordination between multiple agent workers. Our claim\nthat most harness interventions add little on top of a plain coding agent (Section [4](#S4))\nis made almost entirely with a single worker; we do run one coordination ablation\n(Section [4.4](#S4.SS4)) across a small set of coordination primitives on top of\nmultiple parallel workers, but we do not otherwise invest in coordination research\nbetween multiple concurrent streams of work. We defer this to future work, since it is\nits own interesting problem, plausibly with a different set of useful interventions than\nthe single-worker setting, and one that likely requires an even larger compute budget\nthan what we use here to study properly.\nA fourth limitation is possible asymmetry in tuning effort: because Malena is our own\nsystem, it is natural to worry that we invested disproportionately more debugging and\nconfiguration effort into it than into the external harnesses it is compared against,\ninflating its apparent advantage. We mitigate this by treating every harness’s backbone\nport the same way we treat our own: each external harness went through its own\nbackbone-specific verification pass before its canonical batch was launched, which\nsurfaced and required fixing genuine incompatibility bugs in the harnesses’ own code\n(e.g., independent tool-calling failures in both MLEvolve and ScienceFlow specific to\nGLM 5.2), and beyond bug-fixing we additionally ran targeted configuration ablations on\nScienceFlow and Arbor (worker count, resource-admission\npolicy, executor timeout, and early-stopping aggressiveness) to check whether their\ncanonical settings were leaving performance on the table.\nAppendix [D.3](#A4.SS3) reports this work in full; none of these\nablations moved outcomes by more than run-to-run noise at our sample sizes, so we kept\neach harness’s original canonical configuration, but we surface the negative results\nhere rather than reporting only the positive tuning story we have for our own system.\nG.2 Broader Outlook\nBeyond the direct ablation results, our observations across backbones and prior harnesses point at two related hypotheses about why scaffolding is losing its edge, which we lay out here as motivation for future work rather than claims this paper establishes.\nPost-training ate the harness. Frontier LLMs are increasingly post-trained directly inside a coding-agent loop – rewarded for planning, executing, and debugging across many turns of real tool use – rather than only on single-turn completions. We hypothesize that this kind of post-training substitutes for exactly the scaffolding that a plain coding-agent environment (Oneshot) would otherwise need on top of a chat completion (Chat): search over what to try next, recovery from a failed step, deciding when a result is good enough to stop on. If so, the Chat-vs-Oneshot gap (Section [4.1](#S4.SS1)) should track how much of this coding-agent post-training a model has received, more than it tracks raw model scale. We see suggestive evidence for this in the DeepSeek V4 family: the Preview releases show close to no gap between Chat and Oneshot performance, while the final releases – built on comparable base models but with substantially more coding-agent post-training – open up a clear gap, though still narrower than the gap we measure for GLM 5.2 (Section [4.1](#S4.SS1)). If this pattern holds more broadly, it reframes a common motivation behind self-evolving-harness designs, where an agent is given the ability to rewrite its own scaffolding to better match its abilities ([Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib42)): rather than each model needing a harness custom-fit to it at inference time, post-training may already be doing this fitting once, in advance, for every downstream task the model is later pointed at.\nCoding agents as the bitter lesson for test-time inference. A second, related reading of our results is architectural rather than about training data: for models capable enough at autonomous tool use, adding hand-designed structure on top of a plain coding-agent session – explicit search trees, planner/executor splits, multi-worker coordination – has a poor and often negative return (Section [4.2](#S4.SS2)–[4.4](#S4.SS4)), while simply running more (and more capable) coding-agent post-training inside that same plain loop could potentially perform better. This is the same shape of argument Sutton makes about general-purpose search and learning methods outscaling hand-engineered domain structure as compute grows ([Sutton, 2019](#bib.bib40)), applied to test-time inference specifically: a basic coding-agent harness plus a stronger backbone tends to close the gap that a specialized harness had opened at a weaker backbone, rather than the specialized harness’s advantage compounding. We see the same qualitative pattern in our own results: weaker backbones still benefit measurably from hand-designed workflow priors like MLEvolve’s tree search, but as backbone agentic capability increases, Malena’s plain single-session harness closes that gap and matches or exceeds MLEvolve without any of its search machinery (Table [15](#A4.T15), Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2)). Taken together with the first hypothesis, the practical implication is the same one drawn in Section [6](#S6): the harness is not where the durable advantage lives.", "segments": null}, {"item_id": "7f93d63faaf11f733276a443", "source_id": "arxiv-discovery", "publisher_id": "arxiv-unresolved-team", "kind": "feed", "title": "IdeaScientist: Orchestrating Agents for Grounded Scientific Ideation", "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04074", "published_at": "2026-10-02T21:35:55Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-15T00:31:12.308970Z", "media_url": null, "discovered_updated_at": "2026-10-09T18:44:38Z", "discovery_method": "syndication", "version_id": "1c8d31d1a52853a33abbf82a2909b64babf8ea03d6714eb54e2df4ed7128e44a", "text_path": "data/assets/7f93d63faaf11f733276a443/1c8d31d1a52853a33abbf82a2909b64babf8ea03d6714eb54e2df4ed7128e44a/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "IdeaScientist: Orchestrating Agents for Grounded Scientific Ideation\nAbstract\nDespite rapid progress in automating scientific research, generating promising and well grounded research solutions remains a central challenge. We isolate research ideation as a standalone task and build our solution on the intuition that a challenge in one field can often be addressed by a mechanism that solved an analogous challenge in another. Accordingly, we introduce IdeaScientist, which decomposes ideation into gap finding, innovation, and report writing, and trains each role with reinforcement learning. These roles identify limitations in related work, draw solution intuitions from analogous problem settings, and develop those intuitions into complete research proposals. To facilitate discovery of insights across domains, we construct the Svalbard Idea Vault, a corpus of 2.77M decomposed research ideas for retrieval, training, and temporally controlled evaluation. Our evaluation restricts access to literature available before a cutoff date and assesses how closely proposed directions align with those later explored in 15K papers authored by human researchers. On Qwen3.6-27B, IdeaScientist outperforms the strongest open-source autoresearch baseline by 14.0%, driven mainly by gains in novelty. On this 27B open backbone, IdeaScientist even outperforms Claude Code SDK with Claude-4.8-Opus and Codex SDK with GPT-5.4, by up to 5.9%.\n[jiaruil5@andrew.cmu.edu](mailto:jiaruil5@andrew.cmu.edu)††Code:\n[https://github.com/jiarui-liu/IdeaScientist](https://github.com/jiarui-liu/IdeaScientist)††Dataset:\n[https://huggingface.co/datasets/Jerry999/SvalbardIdeaVault](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Jerry999/SvalbardIdeaVault)\n1 Introduction\nAs coding agents take on a growing share of implementation work, Autonomous Research (AutoResearch) has attracted increasing attention ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib5); [Yamada et al., 2025](#bib.bib6); [Gottweis et al., 2026](#bib.bib8); [Schmidgall et al., 2025](#bib.bib10)). Yet most of this progress sits downstream: existing systems couple solution proposal with implementation, experimentation, and writing, making it difficult to determine whether success comes from strong research ideas or strong downstream execution. Frontier labs report the same asymmetry: OpenAI recently reported its “automated research intern” focuses on well-defined tasks under human direction, whereas high-level planning remains a minimal fraction of agent output ([OpenAI, 2026](#bib.bib93)).\nIn this paper we focus on the problem of research ideation, which we define as follows: given a problem definition (the task input and the desired output) together with a challenge that current solutions fail to meet, produce a grounded research proposal that addresses the challenge. The challenge may concern any desideratum of the output or of the process producing it, such as inferior quality, high inference latency, or privacy risks. We ask two questions: (1) how can agents generate high-quality research proposals for open research problems? (2) how can such proposals be evaluated reliably before experimentation, when many plausible solutions exist and none is uniquely correct?\nWhere, then, do research ideas come from? Our key intuition is rooted in a recurring view in Cognitive Science and Psychonomic Science: novelty emerges by connecting existing knowledge in new ways, and a solution developed for one problem transfers to a seemingly unrelated one when the two share the same underlying structure ([Mednick, 1962](#bib.bib1); [Uzzi et al., 2013](#bib.bib2); [Gentner, 1983](#bib.bib87); [Gick and Holyoak, 1983](#bib.bib3); [Loewenstein et al., 1999](#bib.bib4)). We thus draw insights not from prior work in the same domain, but from work addressing a similar challenge in a different field. For example, web search cuts latency by low-cost retrieval of a candidate set before applying a sophisticated ranker; the same two-stage structure answers the latency challenge in recommendation, where first-stage filtering precedes fine-grained scoring.\nWe introduce IdeaScientist, an agent framework that operationalizes this process with three specialized sub-agents. A gap finder examines closely related prior solutions in the target domain to identify the shared methodological limitation that leaves the challenge unaddressed, or even amplifies it. An innovator searches for mechanisms that have addressed a similar challenge in other fields, then adapts them to the target setting. Finally, a report writer turns the resulting intuitions into a complete research proposal ([Baek et al., 2025](#bib.bib9); [Keya et al., 2026](#bib.bib44)). We train each sub-agent with asynchronous GRPO and role-specific, rubric-based rewards.\nBoth training and inference rely on a Svalbard Idea Vault11 1 This is named after the Svalbard Global Seed Vault, as a metaphor for the repository of research ideas., an offline corpus built through idea decomposition, which extracts 2.77 million (problem definition, challenge, research gap, intuition, proposal) tuples from research papers. We set the cutoff date to January 1, 2026, and treat the 14,977 methodology papers published after the cutoff as post-cutoff targets. The idea vault serves three purposes. Pre-cutoff papers form the literature that the gap finder and the innovator search. A portion of the post-cutoff targets generates training data: the agent receives only the problem definition and challenge and produces a proposal, and comparing it against the target paper’s actual solution yields the supervision signal for reinforcement learning. The remaining targets are held out for evaluation—because the agent cannot search literature published after the cutoff during evaluation, this design tests whether it can infer a research direction before that direction appeared.\nIn summary, this paper makes the following contributions:\n- 1. \nWe separate out research ideation from AutoResearch, and formalize it as producing a grounded proposal from a problem definition and a challenge.\n- 2. \nWe introduce IdeaScientist, an agent framework built on the intuition of searching for mechanisms that address similar challenges in different fields, and apply role-specific reinforcement learning to improve gap finding, innovation, and report writing.\n- 3. \nWe construct the Svalbard Idea Vault, an offline corpus of 2.77 million (problem definition, challenge, research gap, intuition, proposal) tuples produced through idea decomposition. The Idea Vault is used for literature search, training data generation, and evaluation.\n- 4. \nWe conduct extensive experiments showing the benefits of IdeaScientist. On Qwen3.6-27B, it outperforms the strongest state-of-the-art autoresearch baseline by 14.0% in overall score, which averages novelty and proposal quality. On the same open backbone, it also outperforms Claude Code SDK with Claude-4.8-Opus by 5.9% and Codex SDK with GPT-5.4 by 5.1%.\nWe believe IdeaScientist takes an important step toward fully automated research, and that it can be combined with automated research-topic identification and automated experimentation for stronger end-to-end auto-research.\n2 Related Work\n2.1 Autonomous Research Systems and Research Ideation\nEnd-to-end autoresearch systems combine ideation with implementation through agent workflows, experiment search, debate, or persistent memory ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib5); [Baek et al., 2025](#bib.bib9); [Ghafarollahi and Buehler, 2025](#bib.bib11); [Ifargan et al., 2025](#bib.bib12); [Schmidgall et al., 2025](#bib.bib10); [Liu et al., 2026a](#bib.bib18); [Song et al., 2026](#bib.bib19); [Tang et al., 2026](#bib.bib20); [Lu et al., 2026](#bib.bib7); [Yamada et al., 2025](#bib.bib6); [Lyu et al., 2026](#bib.bib15); [Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib16); [Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib17); [Gottweis et al., 2026](#bib.bib8); [Su et al., 2025](#bib.bib25); [Weng et al., 2026](#bib.bib13); [Mitchener et al., 2025](#bib.bib14)). Their objectives typically reward experiment selection or downstream success, entangling proposal quality with coding and empirical outcomes ([Falck et al., 2026](#bib.bib37); [Yang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib38)). Recent work supervises ideation more directly through execution feedback, supervised fine-tuning followed by rewards for technical depth and semantic alignment, debate-derived rewards, or paper-specific reference-free rubrics ([Zhang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib34); [Gu et al., 2026](#bib.bib35); [Salimi et al., 2026](#bib.bib36); [Wang et al., 2026c](#bib.bib39)). However, these methods do not train literature search as an inspectable sequence of research operations.\nRetrieval-based deepresearch systems structure evidence use through knowledge graphs, hybrid retrieval, citation paths, iterative search, or outline-guided synthesis ([Wang et al., 2024a](#bib.bib21); [Wang et al., 2024b](#bib.bib22); [Li et al., 2025a](#bib.bib23); [Hu et al., 2024](#bib.bib24); [Wang et al., 2024c](#bib.bib81); [Yan et al., 2025](#bib.bib82); [Shao et al., 2024a](#bib.bib83)). Other methods rank research gaps or recombine ideas across scholarly trajectories ([Shahid et al., 2025](#bib.bib26); [Xu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib27); [Li et al., 2026c](#bib.bib30); [Radensky et al., 2024](#bib.bib31); [Suzuki and Banaei-Kashani, 2026](#bib.bib32); [Choi and Song, 2026](#bib.bib33)). IdeaScientist combines these threads in an inspectable ideation process, separately training agents to identify limitations in closely related work, seek inspiration from how other fields address similar challenges, and develop those insights into complete proposals.\n2.2 Evaluating Generated Research Ideas\nPre-execution evaluation must substitute observable proxies for eventual scientific value, but different proxies answer different questions. Reference-free rubrics use LLM judges to assess whether an idea appears high quality across dimensions such as novelty and feasibility ([Guo et al., 2025](#bib.bib28); [Ruan et al., 2026](#bib.bib29); [Chen et al., 2026](#bib.bib56); [Schopf and Färber, 2026](#bib.bib59); [Wang et al., 2026d](#bib.bib57)). Learned reviewers estimate acceptance-like quality ([Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib41); [Idahl and Ahmadi, 2025](#bib.bib42); [Weng et al., 2025](#bib.bib43); [Zeng et al., 2025](#bib.bib45); [Nguyen et al., 2026](#bib.bib48); [Deng et al., 2026](#bib.bib47); [Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib51); [Camelo-Guerrero and Diaz-Rodriguez, 2026](#bib.bib50); [Akella et al., 2025](#bib.bib49); [Wen et al., 2026](#bib.bib54)). Factuality metrics test whether generated claims are directly supported by cited or retrieved scientific evidence ([Min et al., 2023](#bib.bib84); [Song et al., 2024](#bib.bib85); [Dhuliawala et al., 2024](#bib.bib86)). However, LLM judges can disagree, exhibit self-preference, or reward stylistic features ([Zheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib89); [Panickssery et al., 2024](#bib.bib90); [Li et al., 2026b](#bib.bib52); [Ji et al., 2026](#bib.bib92); [Norman et al., 2026](#bib.bib91); [Sinhahajari et al., 2026](#bib.bib58); [Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib97)). Expert studies also find that greater novelty may come at the cost of feasibility in actual execution ([Si et al., 2025](#bib.bib88); [Ho et al., 2026](#bib.bib46); [Si et al., 2026](#bib.bib53); [Jiang, 2026](#bib.bib55)). Direct execution offers a more direct outcome-based signal, but it is costly and mixes ideation with implementation skill ([Foster et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)). We validate and justify our evaluation metrics through cross-evaluation and manual annotation.\n2.3 Training LLM Agents for Non-Verifiable, Long-Horizon Tasks\nIdeation is difficult to train because unlike code, a proposal has no directly verifiable reward. Rubric-based reinforcement learning converts qualitative criteria into scalar supervision ([Gunjal et al., 2026](#bib.bib60); [Huang et al., 2025](#bib.bib61); [Zhou et al., 2025](#bib.bib62); [Arora et al., 2025](#bib.bib63); [Mahmoud et al., 2026](#bib.bib64); [Liu et al., 2026d](#bib.bib68); [Xu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib67); [Shao et al., 2025](#bib.bib70); [Goel et al., 2025](#bib.bib69)), but a single proposal-level rubric provides weak credit assignment and invites reward hacking ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib65); [Yang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib66)). Outcome feedback can optimize long tool-use trajectories ([Jin et al., 2025](#bib.bib77); [Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib78); [Luo et al., 2025](#bib.bib79); [Wei et al., 2026](#bib.bib80)). In multi-agent settings, however, prior methods either specialize roles under a shared terminal objective or distill their trajectories into one policy ([Motwani et al., 2024](#bib.bib73); [Zhao et al., 2026](#bib.bib74); [Park et al., 2025](#bib.bib75); [Li et al., 2026d](#bib.bib72); [Li et al., 2025b](#bib.bib76)). This leaves unclear which reasoning stage improved and whether specialization survives deployment. To make long-horizon research ideation trainable, we use Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) ([Shao et al., 2024b](#bib.bib71)) to train each subagent role with a stage-matched human reference artifact and rubric, then recompose the trained roles within the full agentic loop.\n3 IdeaScientist: A Framework for Autoresearch Ideation\n3.1 Harness Design\nIdeaScientist decomposes research ideation into three specialized sub-agents coordinated by an orchestrator: a gap finder examines closely related works to identify methodological limitations, an innovator draws on work from different problem settings to develop transferable solution intuitions, and a report writer consolidates the surviving intuition into a complete proposal. The report writer follows a fixed output schema that requires it to formalize the problem setting, state the central novelty, distinguish the proposed mechanism from the closest prior methods, and provide sufficient architectural and training detail to make the proposal actionable. It also specifies an evaluation plan, falsifiable predictions and their refutation conditions, and the proposal’s limitations, while supporting claims about prior work with inline database identifiers. Table [5](#A1.T5) provides the complete schema.\nThe orchestrator maintains the evolving research plan and determines which sub-agent to invoke next, allowing the system to revisit earlier stages when additional evidence or refinement is needed. The specialized agents execute the corresponding stages of research ideation through predefined interfaces.\nBecause full papers are often too long to include directly in every agent context, we separate planning and synthesis from full paper reading and reviewing. A dedicated second-tier paper reader accesses full paper text in a separate context, performs targeted QA, and returns its responses to the requesting agent. A second-tier reviewer evaluates quality of generated research intuitions and proposals.\nFigure [1](#S1.F1) shows the resulting flow from the problem and challenge to a grounded gap, a research intuition that may be adapted from another field, and a complete proposal. These intermediate artifacts let us check and train the gap finder, innovator, and report writer separately. The complete action space for each role is listed in Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1).\n3.2 Training Design\nWe train the gap finder, innovator, and report writer separately before recomposing them in the shared harness, while preserving the same inputs, outputs, information access, and actions during training and deployment. Per-role training is possible because each agent has a clearly defined input and output, while human-authored papers provide valuable information from which corresponding reference artifacts can be reconstructed. This decomposition turns a sparse proposal-level outcome into stage-specific learning signals without prescribing a single tool-use trajectory. We optimize a separate LoRA adapter for each role using asynchronous Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) ([Shao et al., 2024b](#bib.bib71)). We keep the orchestrator fixed because its deliberately narrow action space to maintain the research plan and schedule specialized roles has little room for improvement. Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1) reports the training configuration details.\nRather than relying only on reference-free LLM judgments, we design a reference-grounded reward that evaluates both the standalone quality of each generated artifact and its agreement with the corresponding human reference. The resulting reward provides an outcome-level learning signal for the complete trajectory of decisions and tool calls that produces the artifact. We use three complementary reward components.\n- • \nReference-free score \\(r_{\\mathrm{free}}\\): An LLM judge rates the specificity, methodological significance, feasibility, and evidential support of the generated artifact, measuring whether it is well formed and coherent independently of the reference.\n- • \nReference-grounded score \\(r_{\\mathrm{ref}}\\): A field-level comparison measures agreement with the reference on substantive research decisions such as the core limitations, proposed method, evaluation design, and related work.\n- • \nCitation F1 \\(r_{\\mathrm{cit}}\\): Deterministic set F1 measures overlap between the papers cited by the generated artifact and those cited by the reference, capturing alignment in literature grounding and retrieval.\nThe rubric reward combines these three components subject to a validity gate:\n|  | \\[r_{\\mathrm{rubric}}=g\\cdot\\left(\\lambda_{\\mathrm{free}}r_{\\mathrm{free}}+\\lambda_{\\mathrm{ref}}r_{\\mathrm{ref}}+\\lambda_{\\mathrm{cit}}r_{\\mathrm{cit}}\\right),\\] |  | \nwhere all three weights are \\(1/3\\) in the reported runs. The gate \\(g\\in\\{0,1\\}\\) is determined by structural, task-compliance, citation-grounding, and tool-validity checks, together with a judged methodological criterion for the gap finder and innovator.\nFinally, the training reward is \\(r=0.85r_{\\mathrm{rubric}}+0.15r_{\\mathrm{proc}}\\), where \\(r_{\\mathrm{proc}}\\) is a rule-based term that penalizes protocol violations (e.g., disallowed tool calls, invalid formats, missing required fields). Appendix [B.1](#A2.SS1) provides detailed rubric definitions and reward design choices.\n4 Dataset Construction\n4.1 Idea Vault construction\nWe constructed the Svalbard Idea Vault as an offline literature database using the official arXiv API. The database covers all arXiv subject areas to allow drawing on relevant work from neighboring fields. The collection contains papers published from 1986 through June 18, 2026, with duplicates removed and full text collected whenever possible. For papers collected on or after January 1, 2026, we additionally follow their reference lists one hop and add cited papers that are missing from the database, retrieving full text whenever available. The resulting database contains 4.7 million papers, including 2.8 million (60%) with full text.\nFor each paper, we apply idea decomposition to reconstruct the problem definition and challenge as inputs and three chained reference artifacts as targets: the methodological gap, the proposed research intuition, and the complete research proposal. These artifacts correspond to the outputs of the gap finder, innovator, and report writer.\n4.2 Training data construction\nWe construct training examples from machine learning papers published on or after January 1, 2026. Starting from an initial corpus of 104K candidate papers, we first retained papers whose LaTeX source is available for download and for which more than \\(70\\%\\) of cited works have usable full text in our database. We then restricted the corpus to methodological papers, such as contributing a new method, model, algorithm, architecture, training procedure, or technical framework, excluding surveys, position papers, datasets and benchmarks, and purely empirical engineering reports.\nThese filters yielded \\(14{,}977\\) target papers. We randomly partitioned them into \\(14{,}500\\) training papers, \\(200\\) validation papers, and \\(277\\) held-out test papers, and used the same partition for all three agents. Qwen3.6-27B converts each human-authored target paper into the required proposal schema, preserving the research problem, proposed method, model design, related work, and limitations, while removing all measured experimental outcomes. This results-masked representation records directions that human researchers have pursued without exposing their experimental success.\nWe generate training data using the extracted gaps, intuitions, and research proposal, which provides the skeleton about the research direction; supporting claims and citations must be grounded in papers read through the environment. We validate every artifact carefully before adding to the training set. Appendix [D](#A4) reports details of the training reference construction.\n5 Experiments\n5.1 Experiment Setup\nWe evaluate IdeaScientist-Base and IdeaScientist-Trained on Qwen3.6-27B. In the trained variant, the gap finder, innovator, and report writer use their trained checkpoints. Every run uses a fixed generation token budget per turn and a fixed turn limit for each role.\nWe compare against three families of methods: (1) general-purpose research agents, including Claude Code with Claude Opus 4.8 and Codex with GPT 5.4; (2) open-source autoresearch systems including end-to-end systems such as AutoResearchClaw ([Liu et al., 2026a](#bib.bib18)), Dr. Claw ([Song et al., 2026](#bib.bib19)), and DeepScientist ([Weng et al., 2026](#bib.bib13)), and research ideation systems including ResearchAgent ([Baek et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)), CycleResearcher paired with its DeepReviewer critic ([Weng et al., 2025](#bib.bib43); [Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib41)), SciAgents ([Ghafarollahi and Buehler, 2025](#bib.bib11)), and AI-Researcher ([Tang et al., 2026](#bib.bib20)), with different backbones including Qwen3.6-27B, Gemma-4-31B, and GPT-OSS-120B; (3) IdeaScientist-Base (our harness) on the same backbones, and IdeaScientist-Trained.\nFor a fair comparison, all systems receive the same problem definition and output schema and use the same tools to access our offline paper database for literature retrieval, while the target paper remains hidden and internet access is disabled. External AutoResearch systems and general-purpose agents retain their native agent loops. For end-to-end research systems, we instruct the agent to stop once a machine learning research proposal is complete rather than continuing to implementation or experimentation. Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1) reports the full configuration.\nEvaluation Metrics.\nWe evaluate the generated research proposal for each held-out target paper using two complementary groups of metrics.\nFirst, Novelty measures how difficult it is to derive the proposal’s central mechanism from the comparison literature in the Idea Vault. We compare with three scopes of literature: against all literature (All), against literature before the cutoff date (Cutoff), and against literature in the same problem domain.\nSecond, Proposal quality measures the research proposal on six criteria commonly used in conference peer review: relevance, clarity, specificity, actionability, soundness, and impact. Appendix [C](#A3) gives the judgment prompts.\n5.2 Q1. | 56.5 | 70.0 | 75.0 | 54.4 | \n| CycleResearcher | 49.9 | 48.8 | 10.5 | 36.4 | 96.4 | 86.3 | 72.3 | 69.9 | 59.1 | 70.8 | 75.8 | 56.1 | \n| SciAgents | 44.7 | 44.3 | 9.0 | 32.7 | 96.4 | 85.7 | 68.7 | 66.8 | 55.6 | 69.9 | 73.9 | 53.3 | \n| AI-Researcher | 54.6 | 56.7 | 19.5 | 43.6 | 94.9 | 85.4 | 73.4 | 69.7 | 57.7 | 70.5 | 75.3 | 59.4 | \n| DeepScientist | 57.6 | 58.5 | 15.2 | 43.8 | 94.0 | 85.8 | 77.8 | 74.9 | 61.9 | 70.6 | 77.5 | 60.6 | \n| IdeaScientist-Base | 66.0 | 67.1 | 67.5 | 66.9 | 96.2 | 89.9 | 80.6 | 77.3 | 62.8 | 73.4 | 80.0 | 73.5 | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained | 67.3 | 68.3 | 70.9 | 68.8 | 98.2 | 90.3 | 77.9 | 77.8 | 64.5 | 73.5 | 80.4 | 74.6 | \nTable [1](#S5.T1) evaluates the ideation systems using the metrics defined in Section [5.1](#S5.SS1.SSS0.Px1). Even without subagent training, IdeaScientist-Base is competitive on every backbone. Against general-purpose agents running in their native SDK harnesses, IdeaScientist-Base leads Claude Code SDK on Claude-4.8-Opus by 9.0% on the overall score (77.7% vs. 68.7%) and leads Codex SDK on GPT-5.4 by 2.4% (71.9% vs. 69.5%). Against state-of-the-art autoresearch systems, IdeaScientist-Base ranks first on all three open backbones. Its margin over the strongest matched-backbone baseline ranges from 3.3% on GPT-OSS-120B to 12.9% on Qwen3.6-27B.\nOur reward design enables training to improve all three producer roles. In isolated held-out evaluation, the final checkpoints raise the reward from 56.6% to 66.7% (+10.1%) for the gap finder, from 58.8% to 69.5% (+10.7%) for the innovator, and from 78.6% to 89.1% (+10.5%) for the report writer (Appendix Figure [5](#A6.F5) and Table [11](#A6.T11)). Recomposing the three trained roles carries these gains over to proposal generation. IdeaScientist-Trained improves over IdeaScientist-Base by 1.9% on average novelty and 0.4% on average proposal quality. It reaches an overall score of 74.6%, which makes it the strongest open system and puts it 14.0% above the best baseline on Qwen3.6-27B.\nAcross dimensions, IdeaScientist’s advantage comes mainly from novelty. On average proposal quality, IdeaScientist-Base is on par with or modestly above the strongest baseline, ranging from \\(1.8\\%\\) on Gemma-4-31B to \\(+2.5\\%\\) on Qwen3.6-27B. On average novelty, its gains are much larger, up to \\(+23.1\\%\\) on Qwen3.6-27B. On that backbone, the gain holds against all literature (\\(+8.4\\%\\)), against pre-cutoff literature (\\(+8.6\\%\\)), and most strongly against same-domain literature (\\(+48.0\\%\\)). Being novel relative to a proposal’s own research area requires thinking outside that area’s established approaches; this is arguably the hardest form of novelty, and it is where our method stands strongest.\nFigure [2](#S5.F2)(a) shows that this advantage spans all six domain groups, with the estimated IdeaScientist-Trained profile exceeding Base in five.\nHuman-annotation-based win-rate.\nWe ran a blind pairwise human evaluation on 50 randomly selected papers from the held-out test split. For each paper, two annotators compared the proposal from IdeaScientist-Trained with proposals from other systems on the same backbone, including IdeaScientist-Base. System names were hidden. Figure [2](#S5.F2)(b) reports the win rates: IdeaScientist-Trained beats every other state-of-the-art system by a statistically significant margin, with DeepScientist the only exception. IdeaScientist-Trained also wins more often than IdeaScientist-Base, although the difference is not statistically significant.\nCase Studies.\nWe manually examined differences between proposals generated by different systems, with representative examples presented in Appendix [I](#A9). IdeaScientist-Trained generally makes a tighter connection between the stated failure and the mechanism used to address it. Its key decision rules follow from the structure of the problem, making the underlying assumptions and expected effects easier to test. In comparison, baseline methods introduce plausible control rules with weaker justification for why those rules address the failure.\n5.3 Q2: How Much Does Each Element Contribute?\nSub-agent contributions.\nWe first measure how much each sub-agent contributes to novelty. Figure [3](#S5.F3)(a–b) compares three configurations: the Report Writer alone (the minimal setup that can write a proposal), the Report Writer with the Gap Finder, and the full pipeline, which adds the Innovator. Each added sub-agent raises both All and Cutoff novelty scores, and the weaker backbone (Gemma-4-31B) benefits more from the pipeline. Identifying research gaps first and then innovating on them helps the system reach ideas that are hard to derive from the problem statement alone.\nInnovator retrieval scope.\nWe next vary the Innovator’s retrieval scope, comparing same-domain retrieval with cross-domain retrieval. As in Table [1](#S5.T1), we measure novelty against three scopes of literature. We add a fourth, stricter measure, Mechanism non-obviousness: we prompt Qwen3.6-27B to independently derive candidate solutions from close prior work and score the proposal’s novelty against these derived solutions. As Figure [3](#S5.F3)(c) shows, cross-domain retrieval improves every novelty measure: All (+5.3%), Cutoff (+3.7%), and most strongly In-domain (+30.7%, from 36.3% to 67.0%). Mechanism non-obviousness is the hardest measure for both variants, but cross-domain retrieval still raises it from 33.5% to 35.7% (+2.2%).\n5.4 Q3: Can We Evaluate Research Proposal Quality Reliably?\nTo check whether the LLM-judged quality scores are trustworthy, we compare them with scores from two human experts on 50 proposals generated by 9 systems. The two annotators scored the same quality dimensions as the LLM judges. Figure [4](#S5.F4)(a) reports two Pearson correlations: between the two annotators on individual proposals, and between each LLM judge and the averaged human scores. We make two observations. First, scoring research proposals is inherently subjective, and even the two experts disagree to a notable degree (\\(r=0.56\\)), much as reviewers do in conference peer review. Second, each LLM judge correlates with the human average at a level comparable to the agreement between the two annotators (\\(r=0.51\\)–\\(0.59\\) for top-3 judges). LLM judges are therefore a reasonable proxy for expert assessment.\nFigure [4](#S5.F4)(b–c) further measures two properties of each judge: its agreement with the cross-judge consensus, and its self-preference bias toward generation systems from its own model family. Qwen3.6-27B agrees strongly with the consensus, second only to the frontier Claude-4.8 judge, and the difference between the two is not statistically significant. Its self-preference bias is negligible and lower than Claude-4.8’s, justifying why we chose it as the main judge. Appendix [H](#A8) reports report-level agreement and the full cross-judge breakdown.\n6 Conclusion\nIdeaScientist isolates research ideation as a capability that can be trained and evaluated independently of downstream implementation. Our system identifies limitations in closely related work, searches other problem settings for mechanisms that may address the same challenge, and develops them into grounded research proposals. Using role-specific training with temporally held-out human papers as reference directions, IdeaScientist improves each stage of ideation and becomes the strongest open system in our proposal-level evaluation. Our results suggest that structured agent environments can make scientific ideation amenable to controlled learning and evaluation, rather than treating it as an opaque byproduct of end-to-end autoresearch.\nReferences\n- \nPre-review to peer review: pitfalls of automating reviews using large language models.\narXiv preprint arXiv:2512.22145.\nCited by: [§2.2](#S2.SS2.p1.1).\n- [§2.3](#S2.SS3.p1.1).\n- \nDeepreview: improving llm-based paper review with human-like deep thinking process.\nIn Proceedings of the 63rd Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers),\npp. 29330–29355.\nCited by: [§2.2](#S2.SS2.p1.1), [§5.1](#S5.SS1.p2.1).\nAppendix A Framework Details\nA.1 Action Space and Retrieval\nEach agent acts in turns. On a turn it may perform one or more permitted operations in parallel or hand control back. For the orchestrator, handing back control ends the run; for a sub-agent, it returns the result to the role that invoked it. Table [2](#A1.T2) summarizes these permissions in terms of what each role can access, modify, and return.\nThe orchestrator performs no literature retrieval or paper reading. It can spawn any producer or reviewer sub-agent, view any file a sub-agent wrote, and modify only the research plan. If it attempts to stop before the final proposal exists, it receives up to eight continuation prompts; the run is also bounded by a maximum turn count. Each producer and reviewer sub-agent can retrieve and request readings of papers and can write its working file. A producer that attempts to return before writing at least 20 non-whitespace characters to its required artifact receives up to three completion prompts. The reviewer has no analogous artifact gate.
