Each first-tier sub-agent may request at most 20 paper readings per task and is bounded by a role-specific turn limit. For the reported runs, the paper reader receives one paper’s full text in a separate single-turn context and returns a digest, so full text never enters a first-tier agent’s context.\nRetrieval regimes.\nEvery retrieving role uses the same keyword-search interface over the offline corpus, but the roles are instructed to use it in the two different regimes described in §[3.1](#S3.SS1). The regime is therefore determined by how a role formulates queries rather than by a separate tool. The gap finder is given one challenge axis and instructed to issue four to eight queries phrased in the exact terminology of that axis and of the target problem definition. It re-queries with rephrasings while the returned pool is thin so that the same-challenge, same-problem-definition prior art is retrieved as completely as possible. Its gap schema then requires the near-miss papers and the reason each falls short (Table [3](#A1.T3)), which is only satisfiable from that regime. The innovator is instructed to issue six to ten queries hunting for a mechanism whose logic could transfer to the assigned gap, preferring queries that leave the problem’s own sub-field and span at least three distinct sub-fields. An in-domain source is admissible only when the transfer is non-trivial rather than a rediscovery of the closest prior work, and the resulting candidate must name the source paper, the mechanism, and the condition the transfer relies on. The reviewer searches in the gap finder’s regime, since its task is to falsify the novelty claim against same-problem prior art. The report writer searches in whichever regime the claim it is grounding requires, because it must cite both the related work the proposal displaces and the source of the mechanism it proposes.\n| Role | Information access | Workspace and handoff | \n|---|---|---|\n| Orchestrator | Reads the research plan and all intermediate artifacts; cannot search or read papers. | Updates only the research plan, invokes a producer or reviewer, and ends the run once the final proposal exists. | \n| Gap finder, innovator, and report writer | Search the offline corpus through bibliographic metadata and request task-focused paper digests. | Each edits only its own stage artifact and returns the completed artifact to the orchestrator. | \n| Reviewer | Searches bibliographic metadata and requests task-focused paper digests. | Writes review notes and returns its verdict to the orchestrator. | \n| Paper reader | Receives one requested paper’s full text in an isolated, single-turn context. | Answers the requesting role’s questions and returns a focused digest. | \nA.2 Agent Output Schemas\nEach producing role must write its artifact in a fixed schema, which is identical at training and deployment and is the surface the reward and the deterministic checks operate on. Tables [3](#A1.T3) through [7](#A1.T7) list the entries of each schema and the instruction attached to each entry. The gap finder appends one Axis section per assigned axis, containing a mechanism-level summary of what has been done and a list of at most two gaps, each following the gap schema. The innovator appends one candidate block per call, the reviewer appends one verdict block per reviewed candidate, and the report writer emits a single JSON object in the results-masked schema with every cited work referenced inline by its database id. The orchestrator’s plan file is a free-form planning document and is not schema-constrained.\n| Entry | Definition and instruction | \n|---|---|\n| Gap | One sentence in contrast form: prior methods do X through Y, but Y leaves method-level issue Z unresolved, naming the specific task or setting and the method assumption being challenged. | \n| Near-miss methods and coverage | The closest prior methods by paper id and how each comes close, the shared method-level deficiency across the papers read, and the ids or search trail checked, with at least two ids unless only one real near miss exists. | \n| Methodological deficiency | The one precise methodological reason the near-miss work misses the gap, tagged by kind such as missing mechanism, algorithmic limitation, wrong inductive bias, credit-assignment flaw, training-objective mismatch, representation limitation, or scalability limit, grounded in a specific finding. | \n| Why it matters | Who is blocked and what new understanding or capability closing the gap would unlock. | \n| Evidence route | The observable evidence or comparison that would show a future method closes the gap, without proposing the solution. | \n| Entry | Definition and instruction | \n|---|---|\n| Intuition | One or two sentences stating the single idea in plain language. | \n| Gap it attacks | The specific gap from the gap analysis and the concrete failure of current approaches it addresses, and why closing it matters. | \n| If improving prior candidate | Optional: which earlier candidate is being improved, the weakness addressed, and what changed. | \n| Source inspiration | The source paper or papers by id and the mechanism or principle borrowed, why it works there, and the novelty type, one of new topic, recombination of distant concepts, or reinforcing a weak connection. | \n| How it maps to this problem | A plain-language translation of the mechanism to this problem, without pseudocode, equations, architecture, or hyperparameters. | \n| Why it could work | The reason the transfer is plausible given the source literature, and the key assumption that must hold. | \n| Novelty vs. closest in-domain work | The closest in-domain paper by id and the specific difference from it. | \n| Main risk | The single most likely reason the intuition is wrong or the transfer fails. | \n| Entry | Definition and instruction | \n|---|---|\n| Title | The proposal title. | \n| Topic relevance | The sub-areas and primary focus the proposal addresses. | \n| One-sentence thesis | A single sentence stating the contribution, under 30 words and without metric numbers. | \n| Core problem | What fails and why, why it matters as motivation only, and a concrete failing example. | \n| Key novelty | A short name for the contribution and a brief intuition-level explanation. | \n| Problem definition | The formal setting, the inputs the system receives, and the outputs it produces. | \n| Key terms | Plain-language definitions of the terms used. | \n| Related work | Per sub-area, a mechanism-level summary of prior work, the open gap it leaves, and its citations. | \n| Method | The high-level flow, the modules and their roles, novel architectural elements, key design choices, and any theoretical grounding. | \n| Model details | The base model and the training approach, objectives, adaptation, and key hyperparameters the method needs. | \n| Comparison to state of the art | The related methods and the specific differences from each. | \n| Proposed evaluation | The evaluation setting, benchmarks, baselines, metrics, and ablations the method would use, stated as a plan without measured results. | \n| Falsifiable predictions | Empirical claims the method implies, each paired with the observation that would falsify it. | \n| Limitations | Three to five concise limitations, acknowledged or apparent from the method. | \n| Entry | Definition and instruction | \n|---|---|\n| Verdict | One of survive, revise with the single weakest dimension to focus on, or reject with a short reason. | \n| Evidence | A bullet list in which each bullet cites a paper id with a one or two sentence reason, or challenges a specific source or prior-work mechanism. | \n| Strongest objection | The single biggest problem, stated in one sentence. | \n| Entry | Definition and instruction | \n|---|---|\n| Title heading | The paper title. | \n| What this paper does | One short paragraph of at most 120 words. | \n| Goal-by-goal answers | One block per reading goal, each restating the goal and giving a focused answer of at most 300 words, grounding specifics in the relevant section or equation of the full text. | \nAppendix B Training Details\nB.1 Role-Specific Training and Rewards\nWhy we train roles separately.\nOur initial design optimized the complete pipeline using only the final proposal reward. The base model rarely completed the pipeline, so most examples received no informative signal. Training each role separately avoids this cold start and connects every update to a clearly defined artifact reconstructed from a human paper.\nRole environments.\nEach training example contains the information expected at the corresponding stage of the harness. The gap finder receives a problem definition and challenge axis, the innovator receives a verified gap analysis, and the report writer receives an accepted candidate. Every role can search the offline database, request targeted readings, and revise its artifact over multiple turns using the same interface available at deployment.\nValidity checks.\nRubric rewards are computed only after an output passes the applicable structural and citation checks. For the gap finder and innovator, the rubric reward also requires full credit on a judged methodological-focus or methodological-contribution criterion. Gap analyses must remain on the assigned axis and contain no more than two gaps. Reports must complete every required field. Every cited paper must have been read during the current stage or supplied through a verified earlier stage.\nRole-specific quality rubrics.\nThe reference-free score captures methodological specificity, importance, feasibility, evidence, and clarity. The reference-grounded score compares the substantive fields of an output with the privileged reference while allowing a valid alternative to score well. The citation F1 score measures overlap between generated and reference citation sets. For every role, the complete reward is\n|  | \\[r=0.85\\,g\\left(\\frac{r_{\\mathrm{free}}+r_{\\mathrm{ref}}+r_{\\mathrm{cit}}}{3}\\right)+0.15\\,r_{\\mathrm{proc}},\\] |  | \nwhere \\(g\\in\\{0,1\\}\\) is the validity gate and \\(r_{\\mathrm{proc}}\\) is a rule-based compliance term that penalizes protocol violations, including disallowed tool calls, invalid formats, and missing required fields. It does not positively reward the volume of searching or reading. The gap finder’s reference-grounded score averages its fields uniformly; the innovator and report writer give important fields weight \\(2\\) and optional fields weight \\(1\\) (Table [8](#A2.T8)).\n[B.1](#A2.SS1). Thus a failed validity gate removes the rubric reward while preserving the compliance term. Reference-grounded fields are averaged uniformly for the gap finder and by importance tier for the innovator and report writer.\n| Role | Validity gates (all must pass) | \n|---|---|\n| Gap finder | Valid schema, assigned-axis match, at most two gaps, every cited ID resolves and was read, judged methodological focus | \n| Innovator | Valid schema, one candidate that builds on the given gap, every cited ID resolves and was read, judged methodological contribution | \n| Report writer | All required proposal fields present, every cited ID resolves and was read | \nJudge and optimization summary.\nQwen3.6-27B scores the rubric with reasoning disabled. Invalid judge responses are retried and excluded if they remain unparseable. A failed validity gate sets the rubric component to zero, while the small process-shaping term remains available. We train one LoRA adapter per role using asynchronous GRPO with a maximum policy staleness of one step and an importance sampling correction for the resulting staleness. Sequence lengths and the number of training steps are calibrated per role, and the full optimization configuration is reported in Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1).\nB.2 Training Design Choices\nBefore settling on the final reward and environment, we explored several alternatives.\nFull-text reading over metadata.\nWe compared giving the reader the paper full text against a faster metadata-only digest. The metadata variant collapsed training, with a near-zero mean reward and most rollouts truncated at initialization, against a mean reward around \\(0.5\\) for the full-text reader, so we kept full-text reading and recovered throughput with asynchronous GRPO rather than by shortening the input.\nComparative innovator reward.\nWe also tested a pairwise innovator reward that asks whether each candidate field is better than its reference counterpart, in place of the absolute reference-grounded score. This variant increasingly favored longer and more elaborate answers: its training reward spiked to \\({\\sim}0.83\\), suggesting that the judge rewarded length and elaboration independently of substance, and then repeatedly collapsed. By contrast, the absolute-rubric reward remained stable around \\(0.66\\), so we kept the absolute reference-anchored rubric.\nProcess shaping and validity gates.\nWe keep a small rule-based compliance term that deducts reward for protocol violations such as disallowed tool calls, invalid formats, and missing required fields. We also experimented with richer per-turn process rewards, including a deterministic reward for retrieval overlap with the reference citations and the injection of a privileged-reference rollout into each group ([Liu et al., 2025](#bib.bib96)). These were run as isolated experiments and not adopted into the final reward. We refined the validity gates over time. Gap depth moved from a soft penalty to a hard cap of two gaps per axis. The report writer switched from a reference list with anchors to bare inline citation IDs after about half of its early episodes failed the read-before-cite check. A brittle rule that rejected any numeric string was replaced by a judged penalty for stating predictions as measured results.\nOptimization choices.\nWe tuned optimization for stability under asynchronous GRPO, using an asymmetric probability ratio clip with no KL penalty and generation budgets calibrated to the observed rollout-length distribution. During development we also tried higher learning rates and smaller global batch sizes and varied the number of training epochs per role. The final values are reported in Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1).\nAppendix C Evaluation Metrics and Judge Prompts\nNovelty measures.\nWe use the three novelty names reported in Table [1](#S5.T1). All is a 1–10 judgment of how much the proposal adds beyond the supplied closest papers from the complete comparison corpus, including pre- and post-cutoff work. Cutoff applies the same judgment using only papers published before the target cutoff. In-domain is the percentage of proposals that exhibit structural cross-setting transfer. A proposal receives one when it imports a mechanism from a different problem setting and adapts it through a shared underlying structure, and zero when the transfer is absent, superficial, or unsupported. Thus, unlike All and Cutoff, In-domain is a binary transfer judgment rather than a 1–10 comparison against a separate literature set. The innovator retrieval ablation additionally reports Mechanism non-obviousness, which asks how readily the central mechanism follows from the three nearest pre-cutoff mechanism-side papers and standard techniques available at the cutoff. We implement this as two separate judge calls. The first receives the target problem, challenge, and prior papers but never the proposal, and independently derives candidate mechanisms. The second receives these frozen candidates and the proposal, then measures whether any ex-ante candidate reaches the proposal’s central mechanism without adding a new hindsight-derived recipe. Four scored levels map to \\(\\{0,1/3,2/3,1\\}\\) for trivial combination, routine extension, non-obvious step, and surprising, respectively; an invalid candidate set is unscored and retried. In-domain novelty and Mechanism non-obviousness are averaged over the fixed test-set denominator, with an unscored proposal contributing zero.\nProposal-quality measures and judge models.\nRelevance measures how directly the solution addresses the supplied problem and challenge; clarity measures organization and unambiguous presentation; specificity measures concrete technical detail; actionability measures whether the method and evaluation can be implemented ([Liu et al., 2026c](#bib.bib94)); soundness measures the plausibility and internal consistency of the technical reasoning; and impact measures the importance of the contribution if successful. All are scored from 1 to 10. Clarity, specificity, actionability, soundness, and impact are assessed jointly from the proposal alone, whereas relevance is assessed with the target problem definition and challenge. The primary Qwen3.6-27B judge and the four corroborating judges—Claude-4.8, Gemma-4-31B, GPT-OSS-120B, and GPT-5.4—receive the same prompts and inputs. We therefore reproduce each distinct prompt once rather than duplicating it by judge model. Dynamic proposal, literature, and few-shot-example blocks are shown as angle-bracketed placeholders; line breaks are normalized for print.\nProposal quality: clarity, specificity, actionability, soundness, and impact.\nProposal quality: relevance.\nNovelty: All and Cutoff.\nMechanism non-obviousness.\nCall 1: proposal-blind candidate derivation.\nCall 2: comparison against frozen candidates.\nNovelty: In-domain (structural cross-setting transfer).\nAppendix D Data Construction and Ideation Process Decomposition\nQwen3.6-27B generates the primary reference artifacts for all three stages. A small residual set of target papers, on which Qwen3.6-27B fails validation after regeneration, is completed with GPT-5.4. Table [9](#A4.T9) reports the per-stage counts across the training, validation, and test partitions. GPT-5.4 accounts for only \\(76\\) of the \\(44{,}931\\) stage-level artifacts, under \\(0.2\\%\\) of the total, so the artifacts are overwhelmingly produced by the open model.\nWe fully deduplicate the partitions, ensuring that neither the validation nor test set overlaps the training set and that the validation and test sets are mutually disjoint.\nProblem-formulation leakage audit.\nBecause the problem definition and challenge are reconstructed from the complete target paper, theoretically they could inadvertently retain method-specific clues to the target solution if the LLM that performs this task doesn’t follow instructions well. We manually inspected the extracted problem definition and challenge for 50 randomly sampled target examples, comparing each with its source paper. We found no instances (\\(0/50\\)) in which the input exposed the target method, including through method-specific terminology, assumptions, examples, or problem decompositions that would reveal the solution.\n| Stage | Qwen3.6-27B | GPT-5.4 | Total | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| Gap finder | 14,970 | 7 | 14,977 | \n| Innovator | 14,934 | 43 | 14,977 | \n| Report writer | 14,951 | 26 | 14,977 | \n| All stages | 44,855 | 76 | 44,931 | \nAppendix E Experiment Configuration\nE.1 Training and Inference Configuration\nWe train one LoRA adapter per role on the Qwen3.6-27B backbone with asynchronous GRPO. Each adapter uses rank \\(32\\), scaling \\(\\alpha=64\\), and no dropout, applied to all linear modules except the output projection and the visual tower, which is unused for this text-only task. Optimization uses AdamW with a constant learning rate of \\(5\\times 10^{-6}\\), weight decay \\(0.01\\), no warmup, and gradient norm clipping at \\(1.0\\), in bfloat16 with tensor parallel size \\(8\\) on H100 GPUs. Each GRPO step draws \\(64\\) prompts with \\(8\\) generations per prompt, giving \\(512\\) rollouts per step, and applies two gradient updates per step at a training batch of \\(256\\) rollouts. Training uses an asymmetric probability ratio clip of \\([0.2,0.28]\\) with no KL penalty in the reward, a maximum policy staleness of one step, and an importance sampling correction. Sequence lengths and training limits are calibrated per role: the gap finder uses a maximum sequence length of \\(26{,}624\\) tokens for up to \\(2\\) epochs or \\(800\\) steps, the innovator \\(30{,}720\\) tokens for up to \\(1\\) epoch or \\(600\\) steps, and the report writer \\(49{,}152\\) tokens for up to \\(2\\) epochs or \\(600\\) steps. For each sub-agent, we select the checkpoint with the highest held-out validation reward; the test split is used only for final evaluation. The reported system uses gap-finder step 380, innovator step 310, and report-writer step 194. The judge is Qwen3.6-27B with reasoning disabled, sampled at temperature \\(0.7\\) and top-\\(p\\) \\(0.8\\). At evaluation the orchestrator, reviewer, and paper reader use the base backbone while the three selected adapters are composed into IdeaScientist-Trained. The trained variant and IdeaScientist-Base share the same offline retrieval interface, output schema, and reading and generation budget.\nReading and generation budget (IdeaScientist variants and backbone controls).\nWithin the harness each producer sub-agent runs under a fixed per-turn generation budget and a per-role turn cap: the gap finder up to \\(15\\) turns at \\(8{,}192\\) tokens per turn, the innovator up to \\(20\\) turns at \\(6{,}144\\) tokens per turn, and the report writer up to \\(20\\) turns at \\(16{,}384\\) tokens per turn. The orchestrator is bounded by at most \\(100\\) turns overall. Reading is done only by the paper reader, which runs as a single-turn tool with a \\(30{,}000\\)-token input cap per read and returns a digest. The three IdeaScientist-Base controls (Qwen3.6-27B, Gemma-4-31B, and GPT-OSS-120B) run under these identical caps. The open source autoresearch systems and the general purpose agents run their own native agent loops, and we do not impose these caps on them. Each role is trained on \\(40\\) nodes of eight H100 GPUs, with \\(32\\) nodes dedicated to policy optimization and \\(8\\) nodes to asynchronous rollout generation.\nE.2 Temporal Cutoff and Model-Memory Contamination\nOur temporal split restricts retrieval to papers published before January 1, 2026, although knowledge stored in model parameters cannot be fully controlled. We randomly sampled 10 post-cutoff papers and queried each evaluated model about their contents, finding no evidence of reliable recall sufficient to compromise the evaluation; moreover, the task requires substantial reasoning and synthesis beyond memorizing a target paper. We therefore consider January 1, 2026 a reasonable cutoff that balances model capability with contamination risk.\n| Model | Public release date | Release source | \n|---|---|---|\n| Qwen3.6-27B | April 22, 2026 | GitHub release | \n| Gemma-4-31B | April 2, 2026 | Google blog | \n| GPT-OSS-120B | August 5, 2025 | OpenAI | \n| GPT-5.4 | March 5, 2026 | OpenAI | \n| Claude-4.8-Opus | May 28, 2026 | Anthropic | \nAs a direct screening check, we manually queried each of the five models about 10 randomly sampled post-cutoff target papers (50 model–paper queries in total). Each query supplied only the paper title and asked the model to describe the paper’s contents, without retrieval or an abstract. None of the models identified or described any of the queried papers as known work. We use a title-only probe because supplying the abstract would itself reveal substantive content. This result provides evidence against obvious title-level memorization in the sampled cases, but it cannot detect latent, paraphrased, or method-level memory and therefore cannot rule out contamination.\nWe chose January 1, 2026 as a pragmatic tradeoff between model capability and temporal validity: it supplies a sufficiently broad pre-cutoff literature for this demanding task while reserving papers on or after that date from retrieval as future directions. The training, validation, and test partitions are all drawn from these post-cutoff targets, and the test partition remains held out from optimization. Accordingly, our results should be interpreted as performance under a controlled retrieval cutoff, not as proof that every evaluated model was parametrically unaware of every target paper. Residual model-memory contamination is a limitation of the retrospective evaluation.\nAppendix F Additional Experimental Results\nF.1 Role-Level Validation Reward\nTable [11](#A6.T11) compares each role’s held-out validation reward for IdeaScientist-Base with the reward at the checkpoint selected for IdeaScientist-Trained. All three roles improve: the gap finder by \\(0.101\\), the innovator by \\(0.107\\), and the report writer by \\(0.105\\). Figure [5](#A6.F5) shows the complete training and validation trajectories.\n| Role | Selected step | Base | Trained | \\(\\Delta\\) | \n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Gap finder | 380 | 0.566 | 0.667 | \\(+0.101\\) | \n| Innovator | 310 | 0.588 | 0.695 | \\(+0.107\\) | \n| Report writer | 194 | 0.786 | 0.891 | \\(+0.105\\) | \nF.2 Training Diagnostics\nTraining and validation reward.\nFigure [5](#A6.F5) shows the per-step training and held-out validation rewards for all three trained roles.\nReward components.\nDecomposing the reward into its terms shows that the reference-free score begins high, while the reference-grounded and citation F1 scores provide more room for improvement.\nReward diversity.\nAcross \\(27{,}225\\) gap finder episodes, the median reward is \\(0.603\\) and no reward is nonpositive. The mean within group standard deviation is \\(0.104\\) across \\(3{,}473\\) groups, and only \\(0.4\\%\\) of groups have zero variance. The reward therefore supplies a useful learning signal for nearly every sampled group.\nRollout length.\nAt the selected gap-finder checkpoint (step 380), the \\(512\\) training episodes have a median sequence length of \\(17.8\\)K tokens, a 95th percentile of \\(22.4\\)K, and a maximum of \\(25.8\\)K under the \\(26{,}624\\)-token limit. The median rollout contains \\(6\\) turns and reads \\(6\\) distinct papers; the corresponding 95th percentiles are \\(9\\) turns and \\(9\\) papers. Reward remains essentially uncorrelated with sequence length (\\(r=-0.024\\)), turns (\\(r=-0.031\\)), and papers read (\\(r=-0.017\\)).\nF.3 Generation Failure Analysis\nWe recompute generation failures from the final 277-problem evaluation, defining a failure as a test problem without a scorable proposal. IdeaScientist-Trained and all three open-backbone IdeaScientist-Base runs have no failures, whereas Claude Code SDK has \\(5\\) and Codex SDK has \\(8\\).\nF.4 Checkpoint Selection\nWe trained each role over multiple checkpoints and evaluated more than one checkpoint combination. Table [12](#A6.T12) compares the selected combination (gap finder step 380, innovator step 310, and report writer step 194) with the superseded combination (steps 122, 116, and 80) on the full test split under the same judge and metrics. The selected checkpoints improve valid generation, Citation F1, proposal quality, the reference-grounded score, the validity-gate pass rate, and the checkpoint-selection average.\n[1](#S5.T1).\n| Checkpoint | Valid gen. | Citation F1 | Proposal | Ref.- grounded | Gate pass | Selection avg. | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Earlier checkpoint | 0.993 | 0.037 | 0.791 | 0.259 | 0.993 | 0.531 | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained (reported) | 1.000 | 0.045 | 0.804 | 0.299 | 1.000 | 0.565 | \nAppendix G Resource Use and Test-Time Scaling\nG.1 Test-Time Compute\nOn the same Qwen3.6-27B backbone and harness, IdeaScientist-Trained uses \\(168{,}916\\) output tokens per delivered proposal, \\(11.1\\%\\) fewer than the \\(189{,}942\\) used by IdeaScientist-Base, so its gains do not result from a larger generation budget. Within the fixed per-role turn and token caps, the per-run generation budget still varies across papers. That variation is endogenous: harder papers can drive more turns while scoring lower, whereas runs that fail the validity gate early spend less and score zero. Correlating the report-writer reward with per-run turns, output tokens, or LLM calls under the caps therefore shows no positive association; it is small and slightly negative for IdeaScientist-Trained and near zero for IdeaScientist-Base, reflecting paper difficulty rather than a compute effect. To isolate the effect of compute, we ran a controlled sweep on a fixed subset of 40 test papers with IdeaScientist-Base on Qwen3.6-27B, varying only the per-role turn budget across three levels while holding the reader settings, generation length, and retrieval constant. Figure [6](#A7.F6) shows that raising the budget from the low to the high level lifts the report-writer reward from 0.290 to 0.465 and the completion rate, the fraction of papers that yield a proposal passing the validity gate, from 0.60 to 1.00, while the mean orchestrator turns actually consumed rise from 34 to 55, confirming that the smallest budget is genuinely compute-starved. Additional test-time compute thus raises quality once the caps themselves are relaxed, complementing the role-training gains reported in the main results.\nAppendix H Judge Reliability\nThe system-level agreement in Figure [4](#S5.F4)(b) is computed by ranking the generation systems under each judge’s mean score for each metric family and taking the Spearman correlation with the leave-one-out mean of the other four judges, and with the Claude-4.8 judge, averaged across the metric families. The excess self-preference bias of a judge is the amount by which it rates its own model family’s generations above the other judges’ mean for that system, minus the judge’s average deviation from the other judges across all systems, so a uniformly lenient or harsh judge is not counted as biased. At the individual-report level the judges cluster similarly: the score-level Pearson agreement with the leave-one-out consensus is \\(0.71\\) for Qwen3.6-27B, \\(0.77\\) for Claude-4.8, \\(0.77\\) for Gemma-4-31B, \\(0.67\\) for GPT-OSS-120B, and \\(0.78\\) for GPT-5.4, placing every judge in the same reliable band.\nAppendix I Case Studies\nThe four cases below compare the human method, IdeaScientist-Base, IdeaScientist-Trained, and seven external systems under the same problem definition, model setting, inputs, and desired output. For the first three methods, each table separates the core idea, mechanism, and distinguishing contribution or limitation; the seven baselines are grouped by method family and summarized individually. The accompanying analysis focuses on how directly each mechanism addresses the stated challenge and reports when the evaluation scores do not support a strict ranking.\nI.1 Composable Safety Alignment\n| Component | Description | \n|---|---|\n| Problem definition | Let \\(f_{\\theta}\\) be a language model whose weights \\(\\theta\\) remain fixed, and let \\(C=\\{c_{1},\\ldots,c_{K}\\}\\) be the available safety categories. For each request, the system receives a user question \\(x\\) and a selected subset \\(S\\subseteq C\\) specifying which categories are active. It must produce a response \\(y\\) that follows the rules in \\(S\\), including refusing or adapting an answer when an active rule requires it. It should still answer permitted questions helpfully and avoid refusing solely because of a category that is inactive. Small control components may be trained using examples of questions, active rule sets, and appropriate responses; the underlying model weights remain unchanged. | \n| Challenge | Safety rules vary by user and context, but a model trained for one fixed policy cannot easily switch between them. Written instructions may not reliably enforce several rules at once. The challenge is to make one frozen model apply the rules selected for each request while still answering permitted questions. | \n| Human paper | Core idea — MOSAIC. Represent every safety category with a small set of learned control tokens and prepend the tokens for exactly the rules enabled on the current request. | \n|  | Mechanism. Train only these soft tokens on question–rule-set–answer examples, including harmless questions and repeated questions under different rule subsets; the language model remains frozen. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Policy switching happens entirely at the input, making composition simple and modular: unlike Base it does not alter hidden states, and unlike Trained it performs no generation-time checking. Success still depends on the frozen model composing the tokens reliably. | \n| IdeaScientist-Base | Core idea. Model the enabled policies as a set whose members can interact, rather than adding independent control vectors: a Set Transformer produces an interaction-aware policy representation. | \n|  | Mechanism. Train rule embeddings, the set aggregator, and a FiLM controller on matched rule-set responses; FiLM uses the aggregate to scale and shift selected hidden signals while the language model stays frozen. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Base is the only proposal here that explicitly learns nonlinear interactions among the active rules before steering. This may capture conflicts missed by additive methods, but it needs combination coverage and can destabilize pretrained representations ( [Liu et al., 2026b](#bib.bib95)). | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained | Core idea. Treat composition as constraint satisfaction: learn an acceptable-state boundary for each rule and move the current activation into the intersection of all enabled boundaries. | \n|  | Mechanism. Train per-rule probes from acceptable and violating generations; at decoding time, solve for a nearby activation satisfying every active probe, then continue generation from the corrected state. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Unlike weighted vectors, adapters, or reward scores, Trained seeks joint satisfaction rather than a compromise and minimizes activation displacement. Linear probe boundaries are only behavioral proxies, however, and feasible/nearest projection is not automatically guaranteed. | \n| Baselines: modular adapters |  | \n| AutoResearchClaw | Parses the active rules and routes through category-specific, disentangled adapters; a lightweight router combines the selected modules before frozen-model generation. | \n| DeepScientist | Trains one nearly orthogonal LoRA adapter per category and an input-aware network to weight active adapters, optionally projecting to preserve general capabilities. | \n| Baselines: conditioning and additive steering |  | \n| ResearchAgent | Encodes active rules as conditioning embeddings and guides the frozen model through a safety router, switching policy through the conditioning signal. | \n| CycleResearcher | Learns one logit-steering vector per category, combines active vectors with a policy encoder, and trains first on single rules and then combinations. | \n| AI-Researcher | Combines rules into a hidden-state control vector, removes components associated with general capabilities, and context-gates the intervention strength. | \n| Baselines: decoding-time scoring or instructions |  | \n| Dr. Claw | Trains a separate autoregressive reward model per category and combines the enabled models’ scores during decoding; a policy manager selects the active set. | \n| SciAgents | Classifies a safety action from the active policies and writes a refusal or redirection instruction, relying on the frozen model to follow that instruction. | \nThe human paper composes safety policies with learned input tokens, whereas Base models interactions among the active rules with a Set Transformer and FiLM. Trained changes the formulation more fundamentally: each rule defines a learned boundary over model states, and generation projects toward their joint feasible region. The baselines instead compose adapters, additive steering signals, reward scores, or generated instructions; even AI-Researcher’s projection protects a capability subspace rather than enforcing the intersection of active policy constraints.\nI.2 Adaptive Rollout Allocation\n| Component | Description | \n|---|---|\n| Problem definition | Given a dataset \\(D\\) of question–answer pairs \\((q,a)\\) and a student policy \\(f_{\\theta}\\), the model generates multiple answers, or rollouts, for each sampled question. A verifier compares each answer with the reference answer and assigns a binary correctness reward. The goal is to improve the student’s reasoning accuracy under a fixed compute budget by deciding which questions receive additional rollouts and how their samples contribute to training. GRPO calculates advantages by comparing rewards within each question’s rollout group. The baseline objective also includes a KL penalty to limit deviation from a reference policy. Inputs. A batch of questions with reference answers, the student policy, a compute budget, and the generated rollouts with their token log probabilities and correctness rewards. Output. Updated student policy parameters, evaluated on the model’s ability to answer questions independently. Every method uses training questions with reference answers or an automatic correctness checker together with a student model. A rollout is one answer generated by the student, including its reasoning. Several rollouts for the same question form a group. GRPO compares their rewards to calculate an advantage, which is positive for answers that perform better than the group average and negative for those that perform worse. If every answer receives the same reward, their relative reward advantages are zero. | \n| Challenge | Standard GRPO training typically assigns the same number of rollouts to every question, despite differences in difficulty and potential training value. This can waste computation on consistently solved questions while giving difficult but learnable questions too few attempts to produce a correct answer and a useful training signal. The challenge is to allocate a fixed sampling budget more effectively to improve reasoning performance. | \n| Human paper | Core idea — CoDaPO. Prioritize questions using both empirical difficulty (fraction incorrect) and token-probability confidence, favoring questions that are moderately difficult but that the student appears ready to learn. | \n|  | Mechanism. Generate an initial rollout group for every question, repeat the highest-valued questions to form a fresh second batch, and use the same values to weight the training loss; no teacher is required. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. CoDaPO changes both which questions are resampled and how strongly they train the model; unlike the methods below, it couples allocation with loss weighting. A fair efficiency comparison must charge both rollout stages. | \n| IdeaScientist-Base | Core idea. Treat questions as arms in a UCB-style allocator: empirical success estimates current solvability, while an uncertainty bonus gives under-sampled questions an optimistic chance to receive more compute. | \n|  | Mechanism. Generate an initial group per question, repeatedly assign the remaining budget to the highest upper-confidence score, and train GRPO on the resulting variable-size whole-question groups; no teacher model is required. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. The exploration bonus can sample apparently very hard questions that variance-based rules discard, then decays if evidence shows they are unreachable. The rule is standard bandit machinery and assumes question difficulty is sufficiently stationary as the policy changes. | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained | Core idea. Estimate distance to the learning frontier from partial reasoning: prefix-conditional success separates questions that are close to a solution from those that are merely hard and currently unreachable. | \n|  | Mechanism. From a small initial group, take the most confident trajectory and an intermediate prefix, sample short continuations, and classify the question as saturated, learnable, or beyond reach; use that estimate to allocate fresh full rollouts. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Unlike baselines driven by final-answer statistics or gradients, Trained asks whether a promising partial solution can be completed. Its diagnostic continuations are discarded rather than trained on, and estimates from a small sample and fixed regime thresholds may be noisy. | \n| Baselines: outcome-statistic schedulers |  | \n| AutoResearchClaw | Uses recent success-rate variation as a learnability signal, while reserving some easy questions for calibration and behavioral stability. | \n| Dr. Claw | Combines rollout accuracy, reward variance, and entropy to favor hard-but-learnable questions; it also relaxes KL regularization for hard questions and strengthens it for easy ones. | \n| ResearchAgent | Allocates from early reward variance and verifier feedback, then normalizes advantages within difficulty bands to stabilize unequal group sizes. | \n| CycleResearcher | Allocates from initial confidence and reward variance, and rescales advantages by the allocation ratio to balance gradients across variable-size groups. | \n| AI-Researcher | Uses moving averages of success and reward variance to estimate learning potential, with modified advantage normalization for unequal rollout counts. | \n| Baselines: learned or gradient-based value signals |  | \n| SciAgents | Trains a lightweight difficulty predictor to drive a capped rollout scheduler, retaining within-question advantage normalization for stability. | \n| DeepScientist | Values questions by the magnitude and cross-trajectory agreement of their policy gradients, then assigns both rollouts and loss weight accordingly; this requires per-question gradient statistics. | \nThe human paper jointly resamples and loss-weights questions using confidence and difficulty. Base adds a standard UCB exploration bonus to whole-question allocation, while most baselines schedule from success, variance, entropy, or a learned difficulty signal; DeepScientist is the exception, using gradient magnitude and agreement. Trained alone measures whether a promising partial trajectory can be completed, which directly targets the hard-but-learnable versus unreachable distinction.\nI.3 Capability Grounded Subagent Abstention\n| Component | Description | \n|---|---|\n| Problem definition | A coordinator sends a user request \\(q\\) to a specialized sub-agent. The sub-agent receives the request, its task context, and information about its available tools, data, and permissions. It must return either a valid task result \\(y\\), such as a customer segmentation query, or a structured abstention \\(a\\). The abstention states the failure category and the specific reason the task cannot be completed, such as a missing age field, a privacy restriction, or an unclear request. The goal is to avoid unsupported answers while continuing to complete requests the sub-agent can handle. | \n| Challenge | Specialized sub-agents sometimes give plausible answers to requests they cannot complete because the request is unclear or requires unavailable tools, data, or permissions. The coordinator may pass those answers to users without knowing they are unsupported. The challenge is to help sub-agents recognize these limits and report them clearly enough for the coordinator to clarify, reroute, or stop the request. | \n| Human paper | Core idea — EARS. Teach abstention as part of the sub-agent’s ordinary response policy, covering missing capabilities, ambiguous requests, and requests sent to the wrong specialist. | \n|  | Mechanism. Fine-tune on production requests paired with either valid results or typed abstentions; humans label a seed set and calibrated LLM judges scale the annotations. At inference, the sub-agent decides directly whether to answer. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Unlike registry gates, EARS can learn limits that are difficult to formalize and needs no separate checker on every request. Its decisions are not mechanically grounded in a live inventory and depend on training-data coverage. | \n| IdeaScientist-Base | Core idea. Verify the reason for abstention, not only the answer/abstain decision, so the coordinator receives a minimal, causally relevant explanation of the failure. | \n|  | Mechanism. Train on examples of checker feedback and corrected explanations; at inference, an LLM translates its proposed reason into inventory conditions and revises it until a logic checker confirms both necessity and relevance. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Baseline gates mostly stop at detecting infeasibility; Base adds iterative proof-checking of the stated reason. Its reliability still rests on an accurate inventory and faithful translation from language to logic. | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained | Core idea. Prevent unsupported generation by decomposing the request into required tools, fields, permissions, and operations, then checking every requirement before the sub-agent runs. | \n|  | Mechanism. Compare the LLM-extracted requirements with a live structured inventory; execute only if all checks pass, otherwise return a typed abstention naming the missing or prohibited capability. The basic version needs no training. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. This is an explicit, auditable gate rather than EARS’s learned decision, but it is methodologically close to five baselines below. Its main value is the concrete requirement decomposition; errors in parsing or stale inventories remain failure points. | \n| Baselines: explicit pre-execution capability gates |  | \n| Dr. Claw | Checks the request against a declared capability list and returns a typed failure reason that the coordinator can use to reroute, clarify, or stop. | \n| ResearchAgent | Matches request requirements to a registry of tools and constraints before execution, returning a structured failure category and explanation on mismatch. | \n| CycleResearcher | Places a thresholded feasibility assessor over a capability schema before the task agent, choosing execution or structured abstention. | \n| SciAgents | Verifies required tools, data, and permissions before execution and reports a categorized reason for coordinator rerouting or escalation. | \n| DeepScientist | Checks each request against explicit tools, constraints, and scope boundaries before generation, producing typed abstentions for downstream recovery. | \n| Baselines: confidence-aware and hybrid gates |  | \n| AutoResearchClaw | Maps a calibrated pre-execution feasibility score to risk, then also audits outputs so the coordinator can decompose, reroute, or escalate failures. | \n| AI-Researcher | Combines executable hard constraints with semantic capability matching and an uncertainty gate, returning recovery guidance when the check fails. | \nThe human paper fine-tunes abstention as part of the sub-agent’s response policy, allowing it to learn ambiguous or misrouted cases but tying reliability to data coverage. Base instead checks and revises the stated reason for failure, emphasizing a precise explanation. Trained decomposes a request into required capabilities and checks them against a live inventory before generation. Five baselines use essentially the same registry-gate pattern; AutoResearchClaw adds output auditing, and AI-Researcher combines executable constraints with semantic matching and uncertainty.\nI.4 Adaptive Multimodal Speculative Decoding\n| Component | Description | \n|---|---|\n| Problem definition | A large vision and language model is the target whose answer we want to produce; a cheaper model is the drafter that proposes several tokens at a time. Given the image, the text prompt, and the tokens already accepted, a method chooses how to draft the next block of tokens. The target checks that block in one verification pass. Accepted tokens become part of the answer; when a token is rejected, the verification procedure supplies a corrected token. The objective is to reduce total generation time while preserving the target model’s output distribution through the standard acceptance and correction procedure. The method produces draft probabilities as an intermediate result and an accepted token sequence as its final result [(Leviathan et al., 2023)](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v202/leviathan23a). | \n| Challenge | A draft model that predicts well for image questions may predict poorly when the conversation shifts to a question that needs only text. Poor predictions make the large model reject more draft tokens, while extra drafting work can erase any speed gain. The challenge is to adapt drafting to the current request so that the time to produce the final answer improves across different kinds of conversation. | \n| Human paper | Core idea — TABED. Keep visual and text drafting active as an ensemble, and continuously shift their mixture weights toward whichever method has recently predicted the target model better. | \n|  | Mechanism. Combine the drafters’ next-token distributions; after each target verification, compare their saved predictions with the target distribution and update the weights for the next block. No new router training is required. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. TABED uniquely learns online from past target verification while retaining multiple drafters, rather than making a hard routing decision. This robustness costs extra drafting work even with shared parameters and batching. | \n| IdeaScientist-Base | Core idea. Use the divergence between a drafter’s text-only and vision-conditioned next-token distributions as a step-level estimate of how much information the image contributes. | \n|  | Mechanism. Compute both draft distributions, turn their KL divergence into a gate on visual-feature injection, and close the visual path when it is redundant while retaining standard target verification. No new training is required. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Base proposes continuous visual gating rather than selecting a fixed specialist, making it finer-grained than the routing baselines. Running both draft paths adds cost, and the written pseudocode does not clearly connect the gate to the final draft-token distribution. | \n| IdeaScientist-Trained | Core idea. Route each conversational turn using the target model’s own prefill-stage language-to-visual attention mass as a zero-shot estimate of visual relevance. | \n|  | Mechanism. Normalize the already-computed attention signal, choose either a vision-conditioned or text-only specialist for the turn, and pass that drafter’s blocks through ordinary target verification; the router needs no training. | \n|  | Distinction / limit. Unlike heuristic modality classifiers, Trained reads the model whose distribution the drafter must approximate; unlike TABED, it commits to one drafter rather than maintaining an ensemble. Attention is still only a proxy, and target prefill is not literally zero-latency overhead. | \n| Baselines: hard routing among specialized drafters |  | \n| AutoResearchClaw | Classifies request modality, selects a specialized drafter, prunes visual tokens, and falls back to ordinary decoding when draft confidence is low. | \n| Dr. Claw | Uses a lightweight, training-free context classifier to switch between visual and text-reasoning drafters as the request type changes. | \n| SciAgents | Uses attention entropy and token diversity to label each phase visual or textual, then sends the chosen specialist’s block to the target for verification. | \n| DeepScientist | Detects visual, mixed, and textual phases from token proportions and positions, switching specialist drafters only at block boundaries. | \n| Baselines: multi-signal routing and adaptive control |  | \n| ResearchAgent | Uses estimated modality balance and reasoning complexity to select or blend drafters, with visual-token pruning and a syntax-aware text drafter. | \n| CycleResearcher | Routes from attention entropy and key–value-cache gradients, while adapting draft length to maintain the target acceptance rate. | \n| Baseline: online soft ensemble |  | \n| AI-Researcher | Mixes visual, textual, and hybrid draft distributions, adjusts the visual-token budget, and updates mixture weights from recent verification outcomes without gradient training. | \nThe human paper keeps several drafting methods active and updates their mixture weights from past target verification. Base instead computes text-only and vision-conditioned draft distributions at each step and uses their KL divergence to gate visual-feature injection; its pseudocode does not clearly connect that gate to the final draft-token distribution. Trained makes a hard per-turn choice from the target model’s own prefill-stage visual attention, avoiding a learned or heuristic context classifier and the human method’s continuing ensemble cost. The baselines mostly route from modality or phase indicators; ResearchAgent and CycleResearcher add richer control signals, while AI-Researcher is the closest online ensemble.", "segments": null}, {"item_id": "810ca788d6580a6871563f4d", "source_id": "whytryai", "publisher_id": "whytryai", "kind": "feed", "title": "Flashcards Are Good. “Gap” Cards Are Better.", "url": "https://www.whytryai.com/p/ai-flashcards-knowledge-gaps", "published_at": "2026-10-01T09:22:28Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-14T03:28:57.793364Z", "media_url": null, "discovered_updated_at": "Thu, 01 Oct 2026 09:22:28 GMT", "discovery_method": "syndication", "version_id": "2ec6998b1c53899734b6cb244fefc18311d1635c79e90220c4b2743ae1235252", "text_path": "data/assets/810ca788d6580a6871563f4d/2ec6998b1c53899734b6cb244fefc18311d1635c79e90220c4b2743ae1235252/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "TL;DR\nChatbots can identify gaps in your knowledge, so have them tailor flashcards to your blind spots.\nThe friction\nThe moment ChatGPT came out, someone looked at it and said: “I’m gonna use this thing to cheat on exams learn new stuff!”\nAnd AI companies ran with the idea, positioning chatbots as tutors and note takers.\nI [even did a fun test](https://www.whytryai.com/p/ai-study-modes) of three dedicated AI study modes last year.\nOne obvious use case for AI chatbots is to create flashcards for your study topics. Claude can code up polished flashcard decks, ChatGPT [launched interactive flashcards](https://help.openai.com/en/articles/20001533-flashcards-in-chatgpt) about a week ago, and Gemini Notebook has had flashcards as an output choice ever since its [early NotebookLM days](https://www.whytryai.com/p/notebooklm-guide):\nBut flashcards are only useful if they focus on things you don’t already know, while chatbots tend to generate decks that cover the entire broad topic by default.\nFortunately, chatbots are also good at figuring out what you don’t know.\nThe fix\nThe next time you’re asking [an AI agent or chatbot](https://www.whytryai.com/p/ai-agent-vs-chatbot) for flashcards, try this:\nPrompt: I want to fully understand [topic / URL / attached material]. Help me find and fill the gaps in what I know.\n1. Ask me around 10 questions, one at a time, to gauge my understanding. Have me explain things in my own words, and adapt each question to my previous answer. Don’t tell me if I’m right or wrong yet.\n2. Group my answers into topic areas and show me a table: each topic with a score out of 5, and a one-line note on what I’m missing. End with a concise verdict on what you see as my biggest gaps and ask me to confirm.\n3. Make a flashcard deck that covers just those gaps, with as many cards as each gap needs.\nThis lets the chatbot establish your current level of knowledge and also helps you reflect on your own weak spots.\nAnd when you’re done, you’ll know exactly where your gaps are and have a ready-made flashcard deck to practice those.\nAs a test, I had Claude grill me on my knowledge of puppy training, and here was the verdict:\nI now have an interactive deck of 37 flashcards specifically about my gaps:\nSo Ozzy is in good hands, even if his board game skills aren’t quite up to scratch yet:\nAnd it’s not just for classic student stuff, either. You can use this for:\n- Understanding an online article, research paper, etc.\n- Brushing up on your knowledge of a client, company, product, and more.\n- Completing certifications or online courses\n- …and anything else that requires you to know stuff\nWhether you’re brand-new to a topic or a near-expert, AI will figure out your baseline and see where you still need help.\nDo this now\n- Pick something you’d like to brush up on (ideally a topic you actually know reasonably well)\n- Paste the prompt into your chatbot/agent of choice\n- Go through the process to identify your gaps and create “gap cards”\n- Flip through the resulting deck\nDid you get a deck of relevant cards that helps you practice your weak points?\nKnow someone who could use this? Share it!\nDig deeper\nThe above prompt is great for one-off flashcard decks.\nBut I also built a self-installing “Gap Cards” skill for your AI agent.\nIt remembers your past gaps, can re-test you on them later, exports your decks to flashcard apps like Anki or Quizlet, and charts your progress as you go:\nPaid subscribers can grab the skill below:\nThanks for reading!\nIf you enjoy my work, here’s how you can help:\n- ❤️Like this post if it resonates with you.\n- 🔄Share it to help others discover this newsletter.\n- 🗣️Comment below. I love hearing from my readers.\n- 🔓Support me and [unlock cool perks](https://www.whytryai.com/p/paid-subscriber-bonuses) by going paid:", "segments": null}, {"item_id": "813b9149789f0913c5bfb94d", "source_id": "arxiv-discovery", "publisher_id": "arxiv-unresolved-team", "kind": "feed", "title": "Language Models as AI Research World Models", "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12235", "published_at": "2026-10-08T16:18:54Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-15T00:31:12.308783Z", "media_url": null, "discovered_updated_at": "2026-10-08T16:18:54Z", "discovery_method": "syndication", "version_id": "4e9fb103fdae9cf7e73a08394a425933da944a26fb69499d2c2935840571b3d8", "text_path": "data/assets/813b9149789f0913c5bfb94d/4e9fb103fdae9cf7e73a08394a425933da944a26fb69499d2c2935840571b3d8/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "Language Models as AI Research World Models\nAbstract\nAI research agents automate the cycle of proposing, implementing, and evaluating experiments, opening a path toward recursive self-improvement. Yet their ability to propose experiments outpaces their capacity to execute them in real environments, making outcome prediction a key capability for sustained self-improvement under limited experimental budgets. We investigate language models as Research World Models (RWMs), which predict the outcomes of candidate interventions across research environments. Our evaluation draws on over 2,600 experimental records from nine research environments spanning pretraining, post-training, and inference, representing more than 171,000 H100 GPU-hours of experimentation. Research knowledge acquired from real experimental experience improves RWM predictions of unseen interventions within the same environment (Spearman +0.27), and can be reused across environments. For example, using only pretraining experience from OLMo3, Marin, and Nanochat, an RWM reduces selection regret in the Qwen3 environment by 78% compared with zero-experience setting. These benefits extend to multi-round Autoresearch under a fixed selection budget: RWMs with in-env and cross-env research knowledge increase the best gain achieved by 15.8% and 11.6%, respectively. Ablations across 13 language models used as RWMs show that adding research knowledge can improve intervention ranking more than changing models or increasing reasoning effort alone. These findings support language models as RWMs and motivate accumulating experimental data for future RWM training.\n1 Introduction\nAI research agents ([Yamada et al., 2025](#bib.bib44); [Schmidgall et al., 2025](#bib.bib34)) are automating the cycle of proposing, implementing, and evaluating experiments ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib30); [Karpathy, 2026](#bib.bib24)), opening a path toward recursive self-improvement. Yet their ability to propose experiments outpaces their capacity to execute them: a plausible, implementable intervention is cheap to generate but expensive to test ([Kaplan et al., 2020](#bib.bib22); [Hoffmann et al., 2022](#bib.bib19)), and deciding whether it deserves training or evaluation budget still requires anticipating its actual effects. For example, changing a learning-rate schedule or training-data mixture ([Xie et al., 2023](#bib.bib43)) may improve performance with one model and budget but offer little benefit, or even hurt performance, with another. Under limited budgets, this uncertainty directly affects which interventions can be explored and how quickly effective improvements are found. Predicting outcomes before execution therefore has direct research value: it helps researchers and research agents compare candidate interventions and allocate resources to more promising experiments ([Snoek et al., 2012](#bib.bib38); [Klein et al., 2017](#bib.bib25)). This need spans pretraining, post-training, and inference, motivating a predictive capability that can serve multiple research environments by anticipating intervention outcomes in each of them.\nTo satisfy such prediction needs, we formulate and study the Research World Model (RWM): a single model that serves multiple research environments by predicting the outcome of executing a candidate intervention in its respective environment (Figure [1](#S1.F1)). This perspective draws on world models ([Hafner et al., 2019b](#bib.bib16); [Schrittwieser et al., 2020](#bib.bib35); [Chua et al., 2018](#bib.bib9)), which support decisions by predicting the consequences of actions ([Ha & Schmidhuber, 2018](#bib.bib14); [Hafner et al., 2019a](#bib.bib15)); here, the experimental intervention plays the role of the action, the research environment provides the context in which it is taken, and the experimental outcome is the consequence to be predicted. The research environment specifies the reference model, data, experimental configuration, budget, and evaluation setup. An experimental intervention specifies one or more executable changes relative to this starting point, and its outcome is measured under the environment’s evaluation protocol after execution. Researchers or research agents can use RWM predictions to compare candidates, select the next experiment, and incorporate newly observed outcomes into subsequent decisions.\nLanguage models are a natural candidate for implementing RWMs: they possess prior knowledge of models and training methods, and they can read descriptions of research environments and candidate interventions expressed in natural language, code, and configurations ([Brown et al., 2020](#bib.bib6); [Chen et al., 2021](#bib.bib8)). Prior work has shown that language models can use experimental experience within a single task ([Zheng et al., 2026](#bib.bib48)) to assist optimization and research ([Liu et al., 2024](#bib.bib28); [Foster et al., 2026](#bib.bib10)). The interventions we consider range from hyperparameter adjustments to changes in model architectures, training objectives, and optimization mechanisms, applied in research environments with different foundation models, training recipes, and compute budgets. Because the same intervention can have different effects in different environments, predicting its outcome requires accounting for the environment in which it is applied. We therefore focus on a question that prior studies have not directly answered: can research knowledge acquired from real experiments in one environment help predict intervention outcomes in another? This question concerns whether experimental experience can accumulate into research knowledge that serves multiple research environments, rather than remaining tied to the environment that produced it.\nOur evaluation draws on 2,653 experimental records from nine research environments spanning pretraining, post-training, and inference, representing 171,862 H100 GPU-hours of real experimentation. We distinguish two prediction settings: in-environment prediction (in-env) uses experimental records from the same environment to predict unseen interventions, whereas cross-environment prediction (cross-env) uses records from other environments to predict unseen interventions in a target environment. Our experiment reveals that large language models can learn from real experimental experience: across five environments in the in-env setting, research knowledge raises mean Spearman correlation on unseen interventions from 0.506 to 0.774. This knowledge can also be reused across environments: without any target-environment records, knowledge from other environments raises mean Spearman correlation from 0.628 to 0.729 across five pretraining environments. These predictive gains translate into better experiment selection. When selecting three of sixteen candidates, in-env research knowledge raises mean normalized discounted cumulative gain at rank three (NDCG@3) from 0.599 to 0.785 across five environments, and cross-env knowledge reduces macro-average Regret@3 by 52.2% across five autoregressive pretraining environments. In multi-round Autoresearch on OLMo3-100M, initializing RWMs with in-env or cross-env research knowledge increases mean final best gain by 15.8% and 11.6%, respectively, relative to no initial research knowledge under the same budget of 24 experiment selections (Figure [2](#S3.F2)).\nAblations on OLMo3-100M further show that adding research knowledge improves intervention ranking more than changing models or increasing reasoning effort alone. Across 13 language models, Spearman correlations without additional research knowledge range from \\(-0.362\\) to 0.621; with research knowledge they range from 0.638 to 0.896, so even the weakest model with knowledge exceeds the strongest model without it. Holding the model fixed, raising reasoning effort from LOW to MAX increases Spearman correlation from 0.609 only to 0.648, whereas keeping LOW effort and adding research knowledge raises it to 0.892. Within the tested settings, insufficient relevant research knowledge is thus a more prominent bottleneck for RWM prediction than limited reasoning effort, supporting the accumulation of research knowledge as a central direction for developing RWMs.\nTogether, these results establish the empirical viability of large language models as RWMs, connecting (1) learning from experimental experience, (2) reuse of research knowledge across environments, and (3) value for research decisions. They show that real experiments do more than test the interventions at hand: they produce research knowledge that benefits subsequent research, within and beyond the environment that generated it. This work acquires and reuses such knowledge through in-context learning ([Garg et al., 2022](#bib.bib11); [Xie et al., 2021](#bib.bib42)), providing an empirical basis for collecting research data and training dedicated models. Our long-term goal is to train RWMs on large and diverse collections of research data, enabling them to continually accumulate research knowledge and develop predictive capabilities that serve multiple research environments, so that research agents can direct limited experimental budgets toward the most promising interventions.\n2 Language Models as Research World Models\n2.1 Research Environments and Interventions\nEach research task \\(\\tau\\) defines an environment \\(E_{\\tau}\\). The environment specifies a reference system and the conditions under which experiments are executed and evaluated. Let \\(\\mathcal{A}_{\\tau}\\) denote the agent’s admissible intervention space. Conditions outside this scope of control remain fixed, including those imposed by an experimental protocol.\nGiven a history \\(H\\) of past experiments, the agent chooses a materialized intervention \\(a\\in\\mathcal{A}_{\\tau}\\): a concrete change relative to the reference code or recipe, possibly combining several edits. A complete experimental rollout produces a terminal state \\(s\\), and a readout \\(g_{\\tau}\\) extracts its target gain \\(g\\) relative to the reference:\n|  | \\[s\\sim E_{\\tau}(\\cdot\\mid a),\\qquad g=g_{\\tau}(s).\\] |  | (1) | \nHere \\(E_{\\tau}(\\cdot\\mid a)\\) denotes the distribution of terminal states, which contain experimental artifacts such as model checkpoints. We use score for an absolute benchmark measurement and gain for its change relative to the reference. For bits per byte (BPB), gain is the reference score minus the candidate score; for accuracy and throughput, gain is the candidate score minus the reference score. When accuracy scores are fractions in \\([0,1]\\), their difference is multiplied by 100 to report gain in percentage points (pp). Final performance gain is our primary outcome, with positive values indicating improvement.\nWe study a fixed-reference setting: experiments within each environment start from the same reference while \\(H\\) accumulates. Cross-environment transfer tests whether experience remains useful when conditions held fixed within an environment change, such as the reference model or training implementation in our experiments.\n2.2 LLMs as Research World Models\nA research world model (RWM) approximates how \\(E_{\\tau}\\) responds to a candidate intervention before it is executed. Let \\(M\\) denote a shared RWM that uses the available history \\(H\\). We focus on the gain readout of the terminal state: the predicted gain \\(\\hat{g}=M(E_{\\tau},a,H)\\) targets the median of \\(g_{\\tau}(s)\\) under the rollout distribution in Equation [1](#S2.E1):\n|  | \\[\\hat{g}=M(E_{\\tau},a,H)\\approx\\operatorname{Median}_{s\\sim E_{\\tau}(\\cdot\\mid a)}\\!\\left[g_{\\tau}(s)\\right].\\] |  | (2) | \nConditioning on \\(\\tau\\) allows the same \\(M\\) to assess interventions across environments. History \\(H\\) informs prediction, and the rollout distribution \\(E_{\\tau}(\\cdot\\mid a)\\) depends on the task and intervention.\nLLMs-as-RWMs. LLMs provide an implementation of \\(M\\) by combining pretrained knowledge of models and training with the ability to interpret code, configurations, and experimental records. Given \\((E_{\\tau},a,H)\\), an LLM can draw on this knowledge and evidence to infer how a proposed intervention interacts with the target environment and affects the experimental outcome. This inference may be implicit in the model’s computation or articulated through an explicit reasoning trace. We hypothesize that such inference approximates the rollout’s consequences for the gain readout \\(g_{\\tau}(s)\\). Let \\(\\phi\\) map the raw observations \\((E_{\\tau},a,H)\\) to an input textual representation (\\(\\phi\\) can be the identity map or a compression). With LLM parameters \\(\\theta\\) held fixed, we write\n|  | \\[M(E_{\\tau},a,H)=\\operatorname{LLM}_{\\theta}\\!\\left(\\phi(E_{\\tau},a,H)\\right).\\] |  | (3) | \nIn our implementation, \\(\\phi\\) serializes the task description, materialized intervention, and permitted history as text. We instruct the LLM to return the median estimate in Equation [2](#S2.E2), expressed in the same units as \\(g_{\\tau}(s)\\). Our experiments assess this approximation through forecast accuracy, transfer across environments, and its value for experiment selection.\n2.3 Learning from and Transferring Experimental Experience\nTo study the learnability and transferability of LLMs-as-RWMs, we formulate research experience across environments as triplets. For experiment \\(i\\), let \\(\\tau_{i}\\), \\(a_{i}\\), \\(s_{i}\\), and \\(g_{i}\\) denote its task, executed intervention, terminal state, and observed gain. We write a history of \\(n\\) experiments as\n|  | \\(\\displaystyle H\\) | \\(\\displaystyle=\\bigl((E_{\\tau_{i}},a_{i},g_{i})\\bigr)_{i=1}^{n},\\) |  | (4) | \n|  | \\(\\displaystyle s_{i}\\) | \\(\\displaystyle\\sim E_{\\tau_{i}}(\\cdot\\mid a_{i}),\\qquad g_{i}=g_{\\tau_{i}}(s_{i}).\\) |  |  | \nEach record also retains reference information, measurements, and execution metadata. We deterministically serialize \\(H\\) as part of \\(\\phi(E_{\\tau},a,H)\\), providing experimental experience for in-context learning ([Brown et al., 2020](#bib.bib6)).\nWe consider three history conditions:\n|  | \\[\\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\\qquad}l@{\\qquad}l@{}}\\text{No experimental history}&n=0&\\\\[3.0pt] \\text{In-environment}&n>0&E_{\\tau_{i}}=E_{\\tau}\\quad\\forall i,\\\\[3.0pt] \\text{Cross-environment}&n>0&E_{\\tau_{i}}\\neq E_{\\tau}\\quad\\forall i.\\end{array}\\] |  | (5) | \nIn all three conditions, the model receives the target task \\(\\tau\\) and queried intervention \\(a\\). Gains in source records are measured against their own tasks’ references. For the cross-environment evaluation, source histories exclude each held-out intervention and its near-duplicate group (Section [3.2.2](#S3.SS2.SSS2)). The queried intervention’s outcome and post-execution metadata are withheld in every condition.\nWe vary the sources of records while holding \\(\\theta\\) and inference settings fixed to evaluate learning and transfer from experience.\nComparisons of LLM backbones and reasoning effort use matched queries and histories. Appendices [B.2](#A2.SS2) and [C.1](#A3.SS1) provide the record schema and prediction prompt template.\n3 Experiments\n3.1 Experimental Setup\nResearch environments and evaluation overview.\nOur evaluation spans nine research environments across pretraining, post-training, and inference (Table [1](#S3.T1)). The corpus totals 2,653 records, accounting for 171,862 H100 GPU-hours. We first evaluate whether research knowledge improves outcome prediction within and across environments. We then test whether these predictive gains translate into better experiment selection under a fixed budget and better outcomes in multi-round Autoresearch. Finally, we compare the predictive benefits of research knowledge, backbone choice, and reasoning effort.\n| Research Env. | Model / System | Eval metric | Records | H100 GPU-hours | In-env | Cross-env | \n| Pretraining |  |  |  |  |  |  | \n| OLMo3-100M | OLMo3-100M | mean BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 480 | 8,572 | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \n| Marin | Marin-153M | mean BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 132 | 12,101 | — | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \n| Nanochat | Nanochat-149M | mean BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 132 | 2,584 | — | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \n| Qwen3 | Qwen3-153M | mean BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 132 | 15,447 | — | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \n| OLMo3-190M | OLMo3-190M | mean BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 132 | 5,582 | — | \\(\\checkmark\\) | \n| Diffusion | E2D2-170M | DUEL BPB \\(\\downarrow\\) | 661 | 47,592 | \\(\\checkmark\\) | — | \n| Post-training |  |  |  |  |  |  | \n| Math distillation | Qwen3-1.7B | AIME24/25 avg@32 \\(\\uparrow\\) | 280 | 10,432 | \\(\\checkmark\\) | — | \n| Code RL | OLMo-3-7B | LiveCodeBench \\(\\uparrow\\) | 149 | 60,672 | \\(\\checkmark\\) | — | \n| Inference |  |  |  |  |  |  | \n| Inference optimization | Gemma-4-E4B-it / vLLM | Quality-constrained throughput (tokens/s) \\(\\uparrow\\) | 555 | 8,880 | \\(\\checkmark\\) | — | \n| Total |  |  | 2,653 | 171,862 | 5 | 5 | \nRWM backbone.\nWe refer to the language model used to implement the RWM as its RWM backbone. Our default RWM backbone is Claude Opus 5 with a 1M context window and HIGH reasoning effort.\nRWM settings and baselines.\nWe compare \\(W_{0}\\), which receives no experimental records (\\(H=\\varnothing\\)), with \\(W_{\\mathrm{in-env}}\\), which receives records from the evaluated environment. The RWM backbone and inference settings are held fixed across knowledge conditions. We include \\(k\\)-nearest-neighbor regression (kNN) and ridge regression, which receive the same experimental records and candidate inputs as the record-conditioned RWM. Their hyperparameters are selected using only the permitted history. Each static prediction evaluation uses a single fixed split shared by all predictors. All aggregate statistics and reported improvements are computed before rounding. Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2) specifies the baseline inputs and hyperparameter selection.\n3.2 Research Knowledge Improves RWM Predictions\nPrediction settings and metrics.\nWe evaluate predictions of unseen interventions in two settings: in-environment (in-env), where records come from the evaluated environment, and cross-environment (cross-env), where they come from other environments. We use mean absolute error (MAE) to measure the difference between predicted and measured gains, where lower values are better, and Spearman correlation to measure agreement between rankings by predicted and measured gains, where higher values are better.\n3.2.1 In-Environment Prediction (In-Env)\nExperimental records improve in-environment predictions.\nWithin-environment records improve gain prediction and intervention rankings in all five environments, increasing mean Spearman correlation by 0.27 over \\(W_{0}\\) (Table [2](#S3.T2)). The record-conditioned RWM also outperforms kNN and ridge on both metrics in every environment, which shows that language models acting as RWMs can learn more effectively from experimental experience.\n|  |  |  | MAE \\(\\downarrow\\) |  |  |  | Spearman \\(\\uparrow\\) |  |  |  | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Environment (unit) | History | Test | \\(W_{0}\\) | kNN | ridge | \\(W_{\\mathrm{in-env}}\\) | \\(W_{0}\\) | kNN | ridge | \\(W_{\\mathrm{in-env}}\\) | \n| OLMo3-100M (BPB) | 320 | 160 | 2.93e-2 | 2.58e-2 | 3.13e-2 | 2.03e-2 | 0.62 | 0.78 | 0.67 | 0.90 | \n| Diffusion (BPB) | 536 | 125 | 1.95e-2 | 1.58e-2 | 1.99e-2 | 8.77e-3 | 0.46 | 0.49 | 0.50 | 0.78 | \n| Math distillation (pp) | 180 | 100 | 1.58 | 1.15 | 1.11 | 0.91 | 0.43 | 0.61 | 0.62 | 0.70 | \n| Code RL (pp) | 34 | 115 | 5.98 | 5.45 | 5.02 | 4.17 | 0.48 | 0.29 | 0.48 | 0.62 | \n| Inference optimization (tokens/s) | 457 | 98 | 13.19 | 13.18 | 12.73 | 1.34 | 0.54 | 0.62 | 0.72 | 0.88 | \n3.2.2 Cross-Environment Prediction (Cross-Env)\nCross-environment evaluation.\nWe take each of five autoregressive pretraining environments in turn as the target. \\(W_{\\mathrm{cross-env}}\\) uses 244 records from the other four environments to predict gains for 71 interventions in the target environment. The target environment and reference setting are provided, with no target-environment records. The held-out interventions and their near-duplicate groups are excluded from the source records. kNN and ridge receive the same source records.\n|  | Spearman \\(\\uparrow\\) |  |  |  | MAE (BPB) \\(\\downarrow\\) |  |  |  | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Target Environment | \\(W_{0}\\) | kNN | ridge | \\(W_{\\mathrm{cross-env}}\\) | \\(W_{0}\\) | kNN | ridge | \\(W_{\\mathrm{cross-env}}\\) | \n| OLMo3-100M | 0.69 | 0.56 | 0.26 | 0.83 | 1.11e-2 | 1.18e-2 | 1.65e-2 | 9.28e-3 | \n| OLMo3-190M | 0.72 | 0.43 | 0.26 | 0.78 | 7.94e-3 | 1.08e-2 | 1.19e-2 | 8.56e-3 | \n| Qwen3 | 0.74 | 0.71 | 0.40 | 0.82 | 9.20e-3 | 1.13e-2 | 1.69e-2 | 7.80e-3 | \n| Marin | 0.42 | 0.28 | 0.43 | 0.50 | 9.35e-3 | 1.13e-2 | 1.03e-2 | 8.18e-3 | \n| Nanochat | 0.57 | \\(-0.17\\) | \\(-0.12\\) | 0.71 | 2.79e-2 | 3.61e-2 | 3.57e-2 | 2.70e-2 | \n| Macro | 0.63 | 0.36 | 0.25 | 0.73 | 1.31e-2 | 1.63e-2 | 1.82e-2 | 1.22e-2 | \nExperimental records support cross-environment predictions.\nSource-environment records improve Spearman point estimates in all five targets (Table [3](#S3.T3)); paired bootstrap confidence intervals are reported in Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1). Yet kNN, which outperforms \\(W_{0}\\) on four in-env panels, falls below it on every transfer target (macro-average Spearman 0.36 v.s. 0.63). kNN can struggle to capture how interventions depend on their environment: semantically similar changes may correspond to different modifications under different target recipes.\nFor example, Nanochat already uses Muon.
